Table 1:
The first three tables summarize the gapped vacua and the patterns of non-invertible
S
duality symmetry breaking in the three global variants of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
. The last table — with the same vacua as in the first one — shows the pattern of
ST
triality symmetry breaking.

We can summarize the content of this section in Table
1
.
15
15

15

Condensed lines which are non-genuine are indicated by a superscript
U
, reflecting the fact that they are attached to a
U
surface for the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry. Vacua which are connected by spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB) of the duality symmetry are indicated by the same background color.

We also include a table for the triality symmetry at
\tau_{\mathrm{YM}}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}
in the
SU(2)
variant. Note that all three vacua form a single orbit under the symmetry action, though the effective topological field theories remain the same as in the previous case.

3.2
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{su}(N)}
gauge algebra

Here we generalize the previous analysis to higher-rank gauge algebras
\mathfrak{su}(N)
. We exploit some interesting patterns that were already encountered in the simple case of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
, in particular the correspondence between gapped vacua and topological field theories with a given 1-form symmetry, and, more importantly, the realization of the duality action in terms of simple topological manipulations performed over such topological field theories.

TQFTs with
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{Z}_{N}}
1-form symmetry.

For global variants with
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry, such as
SU(N)
, the TQFTs describing the gapped vacua are completely characterized by the symmetry-breaking pattern
\mathbb{Z}_{N}\rightarrow H=\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. Not all global variants fall in this category: the 1-form symmetry may take the form
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{m_{1}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{m_{2}}
as it may happen in
SU(N)/\mathbb{Z}_{k}
, see (
2.11
). In the following we will focus on the case
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, and then make some comments about more complicated examples at the end.

A TQFT with
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry can be specified by the symmetry-breaking pattern
\mathbb{Z}_{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. The broken symmetry is described by a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}/\mathbb{Z}_{m}
gauge theory, while an SPT phase
\alpha_{m}
is assigned to the preserved subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
.
16
16

16

More precisely, one should also specify the coupling to the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
background field. The choice of coupling can be absorbed by a rescaling
B\to rB
, with
\gcd(r,N)=1
.

The latter is determined by a class

\alpha_{m}\,\in\,H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{m},\,U(1)\bigr)=\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(2,m)\,m}\;.

(3.16)

As generator we take the Pontryagin square operation
\mathfrak{P}\!:\!H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{m})\to H^{4}\bigl(X,\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(2,m)\,m}\bigr)
:

\alpha_{m}(b)=\frac{2\pi i}{2m}\,\mathfrak{P}(b)\qquad\text{with}\qquad b\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{m})\;.

(3.17)

On spin manifolds,
\mathfrak{P}(b)
is an even class and thus there are only
m
SPTs given by
\ell\,\alpha_{m}
, regardless of whether
m
is even or odd.
17
17

17

More precisely, for spin theories these classes should be read from
\Omega_{4}^{\text{spin}}(B^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{N})=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, where the first generator is the signature
\sigma(X)
. See,
e.g.
,
[
18
]
for a derivation.

For
H=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
we have the SPTs

\text{SPT}_{\ell}(B)=\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2N}\int\mathfrak{P}(B)\biggr)\qquad\text{with }\ell\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}_{N}\;.

(3.18)

On the other hand, for
H=\{0\}
the TQFT is a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory with partition function
18
18

18

In order to fix the normalization of the various TQFTs, we work under the assumption that the spacetime manifold is closed, orientable, connected and simply connected.

Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N}](B)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\rvert}}\,\sum_{b\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})}\!\!\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i}{N}\!\int\!b\,\cup B\biggr)=\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\rvert}\;\;\delta_{H^{2}}(B)\;.

(3.19)

For
N
prime, the cases above exhaust all realizations of the 1-form symmetry, and are in correspondence with the
N+1
gapped vacua (
3.3
).

More generaly, for
N=kk^{\prime}
but still considering a global variant with
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, partially broken phases with
H=\mathbb{Z}_{k}
and SPT given by
\ell\alpha_{k}
are described by theories that we dub
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}
with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{k}
, whose partition function is

\displaystyle Z\bigl[\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}\bigr](B)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2Nk^{\prime}}\!\int\!\mathfrak{P}(B)\biggr)\,Z[\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}}](B)

(3.20)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})\rvert}\;\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2Nk^{\prime}}\!\int\!\mathfrak{P}(B)\biggr)\;\delta_{H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})}(B)\;.

The partition function of the
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}}
gauge theory vanishes unless
B=k^{\prime}\tilde{B}
with
\tilde{B}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})
, and in that case the SPT phase takes the well-defined form
\exp\bigl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2k}\!\int\!\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{B})\bigr)
. In particular

\mathbb{Z}_{N|1}^{0}\,\equiv\,\mathbb{Z}_{N}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{Z}_{N|N}^{\ell}\,\equiv\,\text{SPT}_{\ell}\;.

(3.21)

Some more details concerning these topological field theories are presented in Appendix
C
.

There is a correspondence between the TQFTs we described and global variants of the
\mathfrak{su}(N)
gauge theory with 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
.
Both are classified by doublets
(H,\alpha)
where
H\subset\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and
\alpha\in H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}H,U(1)\bigr)
. Indeed the data
(H,\alpha)
determines how to reach a given global variant starting from the electric
SU(N)
via discrete gauging, but also specifies the possible patterns of spontaneous symmetry breaking. As the set of global variants is also isomorphic to the gapped vacua of the
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
theory, one might expect that each
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory is realized exactly once in a certain massive vacuum of
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
with gauge group
G=SU(N)
. This expectation is indeed correct, as we will see.

As mentioned at the beginning, there are some global variants of the kind
\bigl(SU(N)/\mathbb{Z}_{k}\bigr){}_{s}
in which the 1-form symmetry takes a factorized form, see (
2.11
). The correspondence between TQFTs and gapped vacua breaks down in this case, and it turns out to be simpler to fix this data through discrete gauging starting from the
SU(N)
variant.

Assigning TQFTs to vacua.

Let us list the TQFTs that describe the massive vacua of the
SU(N)

\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
theory.
As already explained in Section
3.1
, in the Higgs vacuum
H
the Wilson line
D_{(1,0)}
— which is a genuine line operator — condenses and the 1-form symmetry is completely broken. This is matched in the IR by a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory. We summarize the properties of this vacuum by the triplet

\bigl(H,\;\langle D_{(1,0)}\rangle\neq 0,\;\mathbb{Z}_{N}\bigr)\;.

(3.22)

In each of the confining vacua
C^{(p)}
(
p=0,\dots,N-1
) the non-genuine dyon
D_{(p,1)}
condenses. Since this is not a genuine line, the 1-form symmetry is preserved. The IR physics is reproduced by a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
SPT phase. The standard choice is to assign to
C^{(p)}
the theory
\text{SPT}_{-p}(B)
. This is in accordance with pure
\mathcal{N}=1
SYM, which can be approached from
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
by sending
m\to\infty
and
g_{\mathrm{YM}}\to 0
while keeping the dynamical scale
\Lambda=m^{3}e^{2\pi i\tau}
fixed.
We stress that this choice is affected by UV counterterms of the form
\mathfrak{P}(B)
, which do not affect the physics in the Higgs vacuum because they become trivial in the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory. We summarize these vacua by

\bigl(C^{(p)},\;\langle D_{(p,1)}\rangle\neq 0,\;\text{SPT}_{-p}\bigr)\;.

(3.23)

In the mixed vacua — that we indicate as
HC^{\ell}_{k^{\prime},k}
— the condensation pattern is more involved. The
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
subgroup of the 1-form symmetry is confined, and only the genuine lines
D_{(rk,0)}
which are uncharged under it may get an expectation value.
As reviewed in Section
2
, the generic lattice describing such a process is generated by

D_{(\ell,k^{\prime})}\oplus D_{(k,0)}

(3.24)

with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. To this lattice we associate the partially broken TQFT
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{-\ell}
, and thus the vacua are summarized by

\Bigl(HC_{k^{\prime},k}^{\ell}\;,\;\langle D_{(\ell,k^{\prime})}\rangle\oplus\langle D_{(k,0)}\rangle\neq 0\;,\;\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{-\ell}\Bigr)\;.

(3.25)

For instance, for
\ell=0
the lattice of condensed lines is generated by
D_{(0,k^{\prime})}\oplus D_{(k,0)}
and the TQFT in the IR is a pure
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}}
gauge theory.

Having understood the case
G=SU(N)
, we can study other global variants by discrete gauging. We can reach the theory
\bigl(SU(N)/\mathbb{Z}_{q}\bigr){}_{s}
from
SU(N)
by gauging a
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
subgroup of the electric 1-form symmetry after stacking with an
\text{SPT}_{s}^{\mathbb{Z}_{q}}=\exp\bigl(\frac{2\pi is}{2q}\int\mathfrak{P}(B_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}})\bigr)
phase:

Z_{\left(\rule{0.0pt}{4.91673pt}SU(N)/\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right){}_{s}}(B_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}},B_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{q})\rvert}}\;\sum_{b_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\in H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{q})}\;Z_{SU(N)}\bigl(B_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}+p\,b_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\bigr)\times{}\\[1.99997pt]
{}\times\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi is}{2q}\int\mathfrak{P}\bigl(b_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\bigr)+\frac{2\pi i}{q}\int b_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\cup B_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\biggr)\;,

(3.26)

where
N=pq
.

SSB of duality symmetries.

As in Section
3.1
, the gapped vacua form orbits under the action of the duality/triality symmetries. Here we will mostly focus on the action of
S
duality, while similar statements for
ST
triality can also be obtained.

The condensation patterns of dyons — hence gapped vacua — are associated to Lagragian sublattices
\mathscr{L}_{k,\ell}
of line operators spanned by
D_{(\ell,k^{\prime})}\oplus D_{(k,0)}
(
N=kk^{\prime}
). In addition, there is a natural action (
2.10
) of S-duality on these lattices.
The relation (
2.10
) determines the duality orbits comprising several subsets of vacua, together with the occurrence of singlets labelled by duality invariant sublattices, which chracterize the spontaneous breaking of
S
duality symmetry at
\tau_{\mathrm{YM}}=i
(or of
ST
triality at
\tau_{\text{YM}}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}
) in the
SU(N)

\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
theory.
The local order parameter
\mathcal{O}=\Tr\Phi_{i}^{2}
was described in (
3.5
), and is charged under the action of
\mathfrak{D}
. The non-trivial relations between expectation values taken by this operator in different vacua can be obtained using the modular properties of the IR superpotential (
3.7
) listed in Appendix
B
, as we did in (
3.8
) for
\mathfrak{su}(2)
.

We can also study the realization of the duality symmetry in the IR by inspecting its action on the low-energy TQFTs. For instance, in the
SU(N)
Higgs vacuum the low energy TQFT is a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory that describes the SSB of the 1-form symmetry by the condensation of the Wilson line
D_{(1,0)}
. This is mapped by the duality defect to a non-genuine ’t Hooft line and the dual vacuum is in a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
SPT phase. Below we characterize such action on vacua completely and find that it matches with the UV duality action (
2.10
) on the lines.

Symmetry action on TQFTs.

The non-invertible duality symmetry acts in the IR on the
SU(N)
variant by a discrete gauging
\sigma
of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry. For consistency, this should mimic the UV action of
S
duality on the dyons:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{L}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{TQFT}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{TQFT}^{\prime}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
IR
IR
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

(3.27)

It is simple to show the following actions of
\sigma
:

\displaystyle\bigl[\sigma Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N}]\bigr](B)

\displaystyle=\text{SPT}_{0}(B)

(3.28)

\displaystyle[\sigma\,\text{SPT}_{\ell}](B)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N}](B)\,,\qquad&\text{if }\ell=0\text{ mod }N\\
\text{SPT}_{-\ell^{\raisebox{0.43054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}}(B)\,,\qquad&\text{if }\ell\neq 0\text{ mod }N\;\;\text{and}\;\;\gcd(\ell,N)=1\\
Z\bigl[\mathbb{Z}_{N|N/p}^{\tilde{\ell}}\bigr](B)\,,\qquad&\text{if }\gcd(\ell,N)=p\,,\;\text{with }\tilde{\ell}=-(\ell/p)^{-1}_{\text{mod }N/p}\end{cases}

where
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}
was defined in (
3.20
).
When
N
is prime, the action of
\sigma
on the TQFTs is compatible with the action of
S
on the condensed dyons, since
SD_{(\ell,1)}=D_{(-1,\ell)}
and the latter generates the same lattice as
D_{(-\ell^{\raisebox{0.43054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}},1)}
(where
\ell^{-1}
is taken in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
).

The action (
3.28
) implies that, in a vacuum described by a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
SPT, the non-invertible
S
duality symmetry is spontaneously broken unless the equation
\ell^{2}=-1\text{ mod }N
has a solution, and in that case
\text{SPT}_{\ell}
is invariant.
19
19

19

One can compare with the discussion in Section
3.1
.

This is related to the existence of a global variant
G=PSU(N)_{\ell}
in which the duality symmetry becomes invertible. Indeed, symmetry defects of this kind have been dubbed “non-intrinsic” in
[
69
]
(see also
[
70
]
).

A similar story applies to mixed vacua in which the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry is spontaneously broken to
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. As detailed in Appendix
C
, one finds

\bigl[\sigma\,Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}]\big](B)=Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N|N/p}^{\tilde{\ell}}](B)\quad\text{with}\quad k^{\prime}=\frac{N}{k}\;,\quad p=\gcd(\ell,k)\;,\quad\tilde{\ell}=-k^{\prime}\bigl(\tfrac{\ell}{p}\bigr)^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}_{\text{mod }k/p}

(3.29)

with the proviso of footnote
6
. This formula includes (
3.28
), and is consistent with the duality action (
2.10
) on dyonic lattices. As a byproduct, for
N=k^{2}
one finds

\bigr[\sigma\,Z[\mathbb{Z}_{k^{2}|k}^{0}]\bigl](B)=Z[\mathbb{Z}_{k^{2}|k}^{0}](B)\;.

(3.30)

This shows that
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{2}|k}^{0}
, which is a
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
gauge theory coupled to a
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{2}}
1-form symmetry, is invariant under the full modular group.
20
20

20

Indeed using (
2.14
) and (
3.20
), in general
\tau
maps
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell+k^{\prime}}
, and thus
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{2}|k}^{0}
is also
\tau
-invariant.

In turn, this implies that the vacuum
HC_{k,k}^{0}
— described by the
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{2}|k}^{0}
gauge theory — is invariant under the full modular group and in particular it preserves the
S
duality symmetry.

More generally, the presence of
S
duality-invariant vacua is in correspondence with the existence of TQFTs which are self-dual under discrete gauging of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry. A thorough study of this problem was initiated in
[
14
]
and completed in
[
71
]
, finding the following condition:

\exists\;k^{\prime},s,\tilde{\ell}\qquad\text{such that}\qquad N=(k^{\prime})^{2}s\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\ell}^{2}+1=0\text{ mod }s\;.

(3.31)

Indeed, identifying
k=k^{\prime}s
and
\ell=k^{\prime}\tilde{\ell}
, one can prove that (
3.31
) is equivalent to

\exists\;k,\ell\quad\text{such that}\quad k=\frac{N}{p}\;,\quad\ell=-p\,(\ell/p)^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}_{\text{mod }k/p}\mod k\;,\quad\text{with}\quad p=\gcd(\ell,k)\;,

(3.32)

i.e.
, to the condition that there exists a phase
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}
invariant under
\sigma
according to (
3.29
). Note that for
N
prime one necessarily has to set
k^{\prime}=1
and
s=N
, thus reproducing the analysis above. On the other hand, for
N=k^{2}
there is the solution
s=1
that leads to (
3.30
). Another instance where these TQFTs appear is in
\mathfrak{su}(8)
, for which
k^{\prime}=2
,
s=2
and
\ell=\pm 2
provide solutions. Indeed, the global variant
G=\bigl(SU(8)/\mathbb{Z}_{4}\bigr){}_{2}
has a duality-invariant lattice of genuine line operators generated by the dyons
D_{(4,0)}\oplus D_{(2,2)}
.
21
21

21

The same problem can be studied for a generic 1-form symmetry group
\Gamma
and one is led to similar conclusions. These results will appear in
[
72
]
.

3.3
Duality symmetries on generic global variants

In Section
3.2
we showed that the action of
S
duality on dyons is reproduced by a discrete gauging in the low-energy TQFTs assigned to the vacua of
G=SU(N)

\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
SYM.
For different choices of global variants, such as
G=PSU(N)_{k}
, the technique becomes slightly more involved. In this section we refine our approach from Section
2
in order to construct non-invertible defects associated with generic modular transformations over different global variants. This procedure allows one to detect the occurrence of global variants for which the defects become invertible, and to diagnose the presence of mixed anomalies between the latter and the 1-form symmetry. Unfortunately, a systematic approach comprising all possible gauge-group ranks is out of the scope of this paper, so we will restrict to
N
prime.

First, recall that in theories with a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry there is a natural topological action of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z}_{N})

[
14
]
.
22
22

22

More precisely, the action is
SL\bigl(2,\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(2,N)N}\bigr)
. The
\gcd
factor is only important on non-spin manifolds and it corresponds to
\tau^{2N}=1
, while if
\omega_{2}(TM)=0
then
\tau^{N}=1
. We will thus ignore it in our analysis.

For
N
prime, this group has two generators
\sigma
,
\tau
that we already defined in (
2.14
). Notice that we can also define elements
\nu(u)
as

\nu(u):\ \left[\nu(u)Z\right](B)=Z(uB)\qquad\text{for}\qquad u\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\times}\;,

(3.33)

where
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\times}
is the multiplicative group of integers modulo
N
coprime with
N
. The global variants
G
of
\mathfrak{su}(N)
(
N
prime) are classified by the quotient
SL(2,\,\mathbb{Z}_{N})/\Gamma_{\infty}

[
54
,
55
]
, where

\Gamma_{\infty}=\biggl\{M\in SL(2,\,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\biggm|M=\biggl(\begin{matrix}u&k\\
0&u^{-1}\end{matrix}\biggr)\biggr\}

(3.34)

is the parabolic subgroup of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z}_{N})
. In this matrix representation, the first column is a generator of the sublattice of genuine line operators, while the second column is a generator of the sublattice of symmetry defects. We can choose the following representatives:

SU(N)\;\to\;\biggl(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\biggr)\;,\qquad\qquad PSU(N)_{r}\;\to\;\biggl(\begin{matrix}r&-1\\
1&0\end{matrix}\biggr)\;.

(3.35)

As we discussed in Section
2.1
after (
2.14
), one can refine the classification of global forms by keeping track of the coupling to the background field
B
and of possible contact terms. This is classified (for
N
prime) by
SL(2,\mathbb{Z}_{N})/\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\times}
where we quotient by the matrices
\big(\begin{smallmatrix}u&0\\
0&u^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\big)
with
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\times}
. We can choose as representatives:

SU(N)_{p}\;\to\;\biggl(\begin{matrix}1&p\\
0&1\end{matrix}\biggr)\;,\qquad\qquad PSU(N)_{r,p}\;\to\;\biggl(\begin{matrix}r&pr-1\\
1&p\end{matrix}\biggr)\;.

(3.36)

In this representation, the topological
SL(2,\,\mathbb{Z}_{N})
acts by right multiplication, where
\sigma
and
\tau
are represented by the same matrices as for
S
and
T
respectively, whilst the duality group acts by left multiplication. We used this fact to construct the representatives (
3.35
) and (
3.36
), following the definitions after (
2.14
). The condition that the matrix for
G
is modular implies that the lattice of genuine lines is Lagrangian, while the quotient by
\Gamma_{\infty}
takes care of the choice of lattice generator. With this recipe one easily reproduces the diagrams in (
2.19
), (
2.21
), (
2.22
), (
2.24
), and constructs the ones for higher rank.

In order to define a duality symmetry operator
\mathfrak{D}
for a variant
G
and a duality element
M\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, we must solve the matrix equation

M\cdot G\cdot\Sigma=G

(3.37)

which determines the topological manipulation
\Sigma
that compensates for the action of
M
on the global form. The global variant
G
could be invariant under
M
: this implies that (
3.37
) is solved by
\Sigma\in\Gamma_{\infty}
. Hence the
M
duality defect is invertible in
G
, but the presence of a nontrivial parabolic element
\Sigma=\tau^{n}\nu(u)
implies that the partition function transforms as

[M\,Z](B)=\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi in}{2N}\int\mathfrak{P}(B)\biggr)\;Z(uB)\;.

(3.38)

When
M=S
,
N=2
,
G=PSU(2)_{1}
, and
\Sigma=\tau
(namely
u,n=1
) this just becomes the familiar anomaly between 1-form symmetry and 0-form symmetry

I=\frac{2\pi i}{4}\int S\cup\mathfrak{P}(B)\;,

(3.39)

as was first noticed in
[
20
]
.
23
23

23

For
N>2
a similar interpretation is possible, however now the 0-form symmetry comes with an action
\rho\in\text{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}):B\to uB
making the symmetry a split 2-group
[
73
]
. Likewise, the anomaly must now be classified by an appropriate class
I\in H^{5}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{N}\rtimes_{\rho}B\mathbb{Z}_{4}\,,\,U(1)\bigr)
.

Whenever
u\neq 1
, the rescaling implemented by
\nu(u)
is important because it allows the stacking operation to have an order compatible with
M
. Consider for instance the case of
S
duality and gauge group
PSU(N)_{u}
for
N
prime. Then, in order for the duality defect to be invertible, we need for the topological manipulation
\Sigma
to square to charge conjugation:
\Sigma^{2}=C
. Furthermore, it imposes a condition on the discrete torsion
u
, namely
u^{2}+1=0
mod
N
. In these cases the
PSU(N)_{u}
variant has an invertible self-duality symmetry which acts via the element
24
24

24

Using the matrix formulation,
M=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)
and
G=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}u&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)
give
\Sigma=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}-u&1\\
-u^{2}-1&u\end{smallmatrix}\big)
. The condition
\Sigma\in\Gamma_{\infty}
for the defect to be invertible gives
u^{2}+1=0\text{ mod }N
, and then
\Sigma=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}u^{-1}&1\\
0&u\end{smallmatrix}\big)
. This matrix can be decomposed as
\Sigma=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}u^{-1}&0\\
0&u\end{smallmatrix}\big)\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&u\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)
corresponding to the element
\tau^{u}\,\nu(u)
with right action. It is also possible to check the result directly by using the explicit formula for the
PSU(N)_{u}
partition function (
3.26
).

\Sigma_{u}=\tau^{u}\;\nu(u)\quad\in\Gamma_{\infty}\;.

(3.40)

As emphasized before, this simple representation of global forms does not apply when
N
is not prime (
e.g.
, because some Lagrangian lattices have two generators). In those cases the correct transformation
\Sigma
must be computed by brute force.

3.4
Examples:
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{su}(3)}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{su}(4)}

With these tools at hand, we can provide explicit examples of SSB in the
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
vacua. A detailed exposition of the
\mathfrak{su}(2)
case was provided in Section
3.1
. Here we describe the examples of
\mathfrak{su}(3)
and
\mathfrak{su}(4)
, which are slightly more complicated and lead to some additional important insights. As before, we mostly focus on the
S
duality symmetry at
\tau_{\mathrm{YM}}=i
and make some comments regarding
ST
triality on the way.

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{su}(3)}
.

There are four global variants
SU(3)
,
PSU(3)_{0}
,
PSU(3)_{1}
,
PSU(3)_{2}
which form two orbits under
S
duality. The non-invertible symmetry is always spontaneously broken. The map (
2.10
) among lattices yields

\langle D_{(1,0)}\rangle\;\overset{S}{\longleftrightarrow}\;\langle D_{(0,1)}\rangle\;,\qquad\qquad\langle D_{(1,1)}\rangle\;\overset{S}{\longleftrightarrow}\;\langle D_{(2,1)}\rangle

(3.41)

and thus

\begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{SU(3)}$}}\\[2.5pt]
\begin{array}[]{c|b|b|y|y}\text{Vacuum}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}H&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(0)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(1)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(2)}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{Cond. line}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(1,0)}^{\phantom{U}}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(0,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(1,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(2,1)}^{U}\\[1.63885pt]
\hline\cr\text{TQFT}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\mathbb{Z}_{3}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{0}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\text{SPT}_{2}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\text{SPT}_{1}\\
\hline\cr\text{SSB}\;\mathfrak{D}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\end{subarray}\ \ \ \ \ \begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{PSU(3)_{1}}$}}\\[2.5pt]
\begin{array}[]{c|b|b|y|y}\text{Vacuum}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}H&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(0)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(1)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(2)}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{Cond. line}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(1,0)}^{U}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(0,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(1,1)}^{\phantom{U}}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(2,1)}^{U}\\[1.63885pt]
\hline\cr\text{TQFT}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}SPT_{0}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{2}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\mathbb{Z}_{3}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\text{SPT}_{1}\\
\hline\cr\text{SSB}\;\mathfrak{D}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\end{subarray}

This case is similar to
\mathfrak{su}(2)
, since
N=3
is a prime number. Notice that the
S
duality defect on
PSU(3)_{1}
is implemented by including the topological manipulation
\tau\sigma\tau^{2}
(instead of just
\sigma
). This follows either from direct computation or from the matrix formalism we introduced in Section
3.3
.

The
\mathfrak{su}(3)
theories also have a Coulomb vacuum, corresponding to the partition
3=1+2
. We will comment on the physics of such vacua in Section
3.5
. The case of generic
N
prime is also very similar. In the
SU(N)
variant we have a spontaneously broken
S
duality symmetry unless we are in a confining vacuum
C^{(r)}
with
r^{2}+1=0\text{ mod }N
, in which case both the duality symmetry and the 1-form symmetry are preserved. Other non-invariant variants work similarly, but with some of the TQFTs permuted, while in the invariant variant(s) the invertible action in the IR is implemented by (
3.40
).

For the
ST
triality symmetry the orbits instead are:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(1,0)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(0,1)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(2,1)}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ST}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ST}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ST}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(1,1)}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ST}

(3.42)

In the
SU(3)
variant the symmetry is implemented by
\sigma\tau^{-1}
and we find:

\begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{SU(3)}\textbf{ -- triality}$}}\\[2.5pt]
\begin{array}[]{c|b|b|c|b}\text{Vacuum}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}H&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(0)}&C^{(1)}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(2)}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{Cond. line}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(1,0)}^{\phantom{U}}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(0,1)}^{U}&D_{(1,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(2,1)}^{U}\\[1.63885pt]
\hline\cr\text{TQFT}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\mathbb{Z}_{3}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{0}&\text{SPT}_{2}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{1}\\
\hline\cr\text{SSB}\;\mathfrak{D}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\text{\texttimes}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\end{subarray}

This is consistent with the action on dyons.

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{su}(4)}
.

Since
N=4
is not prime, the
\mathfrak{su}(4)
theory has two new gapped vacua:
HC_{2,2}^{(0)}
and
HC_{2,2}^{(1)}
which correspond to the condensation of the lattices of dyons
\langle D_{(2,0)}\oplus D_{(0,2)}\rangle
and
\langle D_{(1,2)}\rangle
, respectively. According to (
2.10
), their orbits under
S
are:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(1,0)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(1,1)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(2,1)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(0,1)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(3,1)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(1,2)}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D_{(2,0)}\oplus D_{(0,2)}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}

(3.43)

By assigning the corresponding TQFTs to these vacua, we find the following table:

\begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{SU(4)}$}}\\[2.5pt]
\begin{array}[]{c|b|b|y|g|y|g|c}\text{Vacuum}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}H&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(0)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(1)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}C^{(2)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(3)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}HC_{2,2}^{(1)}&HC_{2,2}^{(0)}\\[0.81949pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{Cond. line}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(1,0)}^{\phantom{U}}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(0,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(1,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(2,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(3,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(1,2)}^{U}&D_{(2,0)}^{\phantom{U}}\oplus D_{(0,2)}^{U}\\[1.63885pt]
\hline\cr\text{TQFT}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\mathbb{Z}_{4}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{0}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\text{SPT}_{3}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\text{SPT}_{2}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\text{SPT}_{1}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{1}&\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\hline\cr\text{SSB}\;\mathfrak{D}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\text{\texttimes}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\end{subarray}

The theory
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}
was defined in (
3.20
). Notice the presence of phases in which
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
is spontaneously broken to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. To show that the vacua transform in the correct way under gauging the 1-form symmetry, we use the results of Section
3.2
. For the doublets
\{H,C^{(0)}\}
and
\{C^{(1)},C^{(3)}\}
it follows directly from the first three lines in (
3.28
). Regarding the doublet
\{C^{(2)},HC_{2,2}^{(1)}\}
and the singlet
HC_{2,2}^{(0)}
, their relation follows from the last line of (
3.28
) and from (
3.29
). In particular, notice that

[\sigma\;\text{SPT}_{2}](B)=Z\bigl[\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{1}\bigr](B)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigl[\sigma\,Z[\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{0}]\bigr](B)=Z[\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{0}](B)\;.

(3.44)

There are three other variants to consider, of which two are obtained from the table above by gauging
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
with a discrete torsion, and the last one by gauging only the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup. The former two give the following configurations:

\begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{PSU(4)_{1}}$}}\\[2.5pt]
\begin{array}[]{c|b|b|y|g|y|g|c}\text{Vacuum}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}H&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(0)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(1)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}C^{(2)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(3)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}HC_{2,2}^{(1)}&HC_{2,2}^{(0)}\\[0.81949pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{Cond. line}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(1,0)}^{U}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(0,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(1,1)}^{\phantom{U}}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(2,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(3,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(1,2)}^{U}&D_{(2,2)}^{\phantom{U}}\oplus D_{(0,2)}^{U}\\[1.63885pt]
\hline\cr\text{TQFT}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{0}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{3}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\mathbb{Z}_{4}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\text{SPT}_{1}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{1}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\text{SPT}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\hline\cr\text{SSB}\;\mathfrak{D}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\text{\texttimes}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\end{subarray}

and

\begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{PSU(4)_{2}}$}}\\[2.5pt]
\begin{array}[]{c|b|b|y|g|y|g|c}\text{Vacuum}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}H&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(0)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(1)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}C^{(2)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(3)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}HC_{2,2}^{(1)}&HC_{2,2}^{(0)}\\[0.81949pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{Cond. line}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(1,0)}^{U}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(0,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(1,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(2,1)}^{\phantom{U}}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(3,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(1,2)}^{U}&D_{(2,0)}^{U}\oplus D_{(0,2)}^{\phantom{U}}\\[1.63885pt]
\hline\cr\text{TQFT}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\text{SPT}_{0}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{1}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\text{SPT}_{3}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\mathbb{Z}_{4}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\text{SPT}_{1}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\text{SPT}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{0}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\hline\cr\text{SSB}\;\mathfrak{D}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\text{\texttimes}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\end{subarray}

On the first one, the IR
S
duality symmetry acts through
\Sigma\bigl(PSU(4)_{1}\bigr)=\sigma\tau^{2}\sigma\tau
while on the second one it acts through
\Sigma\bigl(PSU(4)_{2}\bigr)=\sigma\tau^{2}\sigma\tau^{2}\sigma
.

Not surprisingly, the most involved case is the one in which the duality symmetry is invertible corresponding to the global variant
[SU(4)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{0}
. To find the table of vacua we must gauge the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
is a non-trivial extension

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{4}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow 1\;,

(3.45)

then the backgrounds
C_{\mathrm{e}}
for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup and
B_{\mathrm{e}}
for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
quotient are related by

\delta C_{\mathrm{e}}=\beta(B_{\mathrm{e}})\;,

(3.46)

with
\beta
the Bockstein map associated to the above sequence (explicitly
\beta(B_{\mathrm{e}})=\frac{1}{2}\delta B_{\mathrm{e}}
). According to
[
74
]
, gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup results in a theory with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
1-form symmetry and a mixed anomaly

I=\pi i\int B_{\mathrm{m}}\;\beta(B_{\mathrm{e}})=\pi i\int B_{\mathrm{e}}\;\beta(B_{\mathrm{m}})\;,

(3.47)

where
B_{\mathrm{m}}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is the gauge field for the dual 1-form symmetry. The mixed anomaly (
3.47
) cannot be matched by a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SPT phase, so we expect the
[SU(4)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{0}
vacua in
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
to always display (partial) SSB of the 1-form symmetry. The theory also has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
0-form symmetry
\mathfrak{s}
that acts as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
automorphism exchanging
B_{\mathrm{e}}
with
B_{\mathrm{m}}
.

Let us first give the table of the results and then describe in detail some subtleties of the gauging procedure. We find

\begin{subarray}{c}\scalebox{1}{\mbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{[SU(4)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{0}}$}}\\[2.5pt]
\begin{array}[]{c|b|b|y|g|y|g|c}\text{Vacuum}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}H&\pagecolor{LightCyan}C^{(0)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(1)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}C^{(2)}&\pagecolor{YellowT}C^{(3)}&\pagecolor{GreenT}HC_{2,2}^{(1)}&HC_{2,2}^{(0)}\\[0.81949pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{Cond. line}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(1,0)}^{U}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}D_{(0,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(1,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(2,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{YellowT}D_{(3,1)}^{U}&\pagecolor{GreenT}D_{(1,2)}^{U}&D_{(2,0)}^{\phantom{U}}\oplus D_{(0,2)}^{\phantom{U}}\\[1.63885pt]
\hline\cr\text{TQFT}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\pagecolor{YellowT}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\pagecolor{GreenT}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\hline\cr\text{SSB}\;\mathfrak{D}&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{LightCyan}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\pagecolor{YellowT}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\checkmark&\pagecolor{GreenT}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}\checkmark&\text{\texttimes}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\end{subarray}

The lattices generated by the condensed dyons in each vacuum always contains at least one local line (not counting the identity). This is consistent with the fact that at least a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
must be spontaneously broken. In the last vacuum, in which we have a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
theory, the duality symmetry is unbroken, but it acts nontrivially as an outer automorphisms exchanging the two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
factors.
To understand the matching of the mixed anomaly we need to carefully follow the discrete gauging procedure and understand the coupling of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry with the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge theory.
We describe
C_{e}
in (
3.46
) as a torsor over
H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
, by choosing a splitting
C_{\mathrm{e}}=C_{\mathrm{e}}^{\beta}+C_{\mathrm{e}}^{\prime}
, with
C_{\mathrm{e}}^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
\delta C_{\mathrm{e}}^{\beta}=\beta(B_{\mathrm{e}})
. We can gauge the 1-form symmetry as usual by summing over
C_{\mathrm{e}}^{\prime}
. This means that

\left[\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cdot Z\right](B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})}}\sum_{c^{\prime}\,\in\,H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{2})}e^{\pi i\int c\,\cup B_{\mathrm{m}}}\;Z(B_{\mathrm{e}}+2c)\;,\quad c=C^{\beta}_{\mathrm{e}}+c^{\prime}\,.

(3.48)

The fact that the above has a mixed
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
anomaly follows from considering the gauge transformation
B_{\mathrm{m}}\to B_{\mathrm{m}}+\delta\lambda
. This kind of mixed anomaly allows for a Bardeen-like counterterm:

2\pi i\,K(B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=\frac{2\pi i}{4}\int B_{\mathrm{e}}\,\cup\,B_{\mathrm{m}}\;.

(3.49)

With this notation we find:

S\cdot Z_{[SU(4)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{0}}(B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=Z_{[SU(4)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{0}}(B_{\mathrm{m}},B_{\mathrm{e}})\;e^{2\pi i\;K(B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})}\equiv\mathfrak{s}\cdot Z_{[SU(4)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{0}}(B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})

(3.50)

where we have chosen to include the counterterm in the definition of
\mathfrak{s}
. Thus the invertible
S
duality is implemented by the discrete
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry. This is the action that we must match in the IR vacua.

To consistently assign vacua, we must compute the action of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauging on the
SU(4)
TQFTs. We find

\left[\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cdot\text{SPT}_{k}\right](B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=\sqrt{H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{2})}\;\delta_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(B_{\mathrm{m}}+kB_{\mathrm{e}})\;e^{\frac{\pi ik}{4}\int\mathfrak{P}(B_{\mathrm{e}})+\pi i\int C^{\beta}_{\mathrm{e}}\cup(B_{\mathrm{m}}+kB_{\mathrm{e}})}\;.

(3.51)

This is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge theory coupled to
B_{\mathrm{e}}+kB_{\mathrm{m}}
. The Pontryagin square term is in principle ill-defined, however this is resolved by the term
kC_{\mathrm{e}}^{\beta}\cup B_{\mathrm{e}}
. The mixed anomaly is instead encoded in the coupling
C_{\mathrm{e}}^{\beta}\cup B_{\mathrm{m}}
.
On the other hand

\left[\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cdot\mathbb{Z}_{4}\right](B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=\sqrt{H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{2})}\;\delta_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(B_{\mathrm{e}})\;e^{\pi i\int C^{\beta}_{\mathrm{e}}\cup B_{\mathrm{m}}+2\pi i\,K(B_{\mathrm{e}},\,B_{\mathrm{m}})}

(3.52)

is the image under
\mathfrak{s}
of
\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\cdot\text{SPT}_{0}
. To see it explicitly, we use the counterterm to write
\pi i\int C^{\beta}_{\mathrm{e}}\cup B_{\mathrm{m}}+2\pi i\,K(B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=\pi i\int C_{\mathrm{m}}^{\beta}\cup B_{\mathrm{e}}
, with
\delta C_{\mathrm{m}}^{\beta}=\beta(B_{\mathrm{m}})
. We also have that

\left[\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cdot\mathbb{Z}_{4|2}^{1}\right](B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=\sqrt{H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{2})}\;\delta_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(B_{\mathrm{e}})e^{\frac{\pi i}{2}\int\mathfrak{P}(B_{\mathrm{m}})+\pi i\int C_{\mathrm{m}}^{\beta}\cup B_{\mathrm{e}}}\;,

(3.53)

describing the action of
\mathfrak{s}
on
\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\!\cdot\text{SPT}_{2}
. Finally,

\left[\sigma_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cdot\mathbb{Z}_{2}\right](B_{\mathrm{e}},B_{\mathrm{m}})=\bigl\lvert H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})\bigr\rvert\;\delta_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(B_{\mathrm{e}})\;\delta_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(B_{\mathrm{m}})\;e^{\pi i\int C_{\mathrm{e}}^{\beta}\cup B_{\mathrm{m}}}

(3.54)

is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge theory which is invariant under
\mathfrak{s}
.

3.5
Comments on Coulomb vacua

All
\mathfrak{su}(N)
theories with
N\geq 3
also have gapless Coulomb vacua. Such vacua often appear dressed by SPT phases or discrete gauge theories that follow from the precise patterns of Higgsing or confinement. Focusing on the pure Coulomb vacuum — which is labeled by the partition
N=1+(N-1)
and in which the
SU(N)
symmetry is Higgsed to
U(1)
— since at
\tau_{\mathrm{YM}}=i
there is a non-invertible self-duality symmetry in the UV, this must also be true in the Coulomb vacuum.

It is known that pure Maxwell theory hosts a wealth of non-invertible defects coming from the composition of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
modular transformations with the gauging of a discrete subgroup
\Gamma\subset U(1)^{[1]}_{\mathrm{e}}\times U(1)^{[1]}_{\mathrm{m}}
of the 1-form symmetry
[
14
,
75
]
.
Once the subgroup
\Gamma
and the modular transformation
M
are identified, this fixes the Coulomb coupling
\tau_{\text{C}}
of the Maxwell theory. In a pure Coulomb vacuum the 1-form symmetry is spontaneously broken, while the duality symmetry is preserved.
Here
M=S
, while the choice of
\Gamma
is dictated by the UV global variant
G
, with the convention that the 1-form symmetry of the
SU(N)
variant is associated to the subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{N,\mathrm{e}}^{[1]}\subset U(1)^{[1]}_{\mathrm{e}}
generated by

U_{l}=e^{\frac{il}{N}\!\int\!\tilde{F}}\;,\qquad\qquad\tilde{F}=\frac{1}{e^{2}}\,{\star F}+\frac{i\theta}{2\pi}\,F\;.

(3.55)

Gauging the electric 1-form symmetry projects onto Wilson lines
W_{n}
with
n=Nm
but also allows for fractionalized ’t Hooft lines
T_{m^{\prime}/N}
. We can restore the standard quantization by defining
A=\frac{1}{N}A^{\prime}
and
\tilde{A}=N\tilde{A}^{\prime}
for the dual gauge field. The action in the
A^{\prime}
variables has a coupling
\tau^{\prime}_{\text{C}}=\tau_{\text{C}}/N^{2}
. By composing with
S
duality, one finds that a duality symmetry defect exists if

\tau_{\text{C}}=-\frac{N^{2}}{\tau_{\text{C}}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\tau_{\text{C}}^{(\mathrm{e})}=iN\;.

(3.56)

The magnetic gauging can be treated similarly, finding
\tau_{\text{C}}^{(\mathrm{m})}=i/N
.
The above argument fixes the Maxwell coupling
\tau_{\text{C}}=iN
in the pure Coulomb vacuum of the
SU(N)

\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
theory.

Other global variants can be treated using the appropriate discrete gauging. We can do it directly at the level of the action by writing it as follows:

S=\int\biggl[\frac{1}{2e^{2}}(dA-b)\wedge\star(dA-b)+\frac{i}{2\pi}G\wedge(Nb-dl)+\frac{iNp}{4\pi}\,b\wedge b+\frac{iN}{2\pi}b\wedge C\biggr]\;.

(3.57)

Here
b
is the dynamical
\mathbb{Z}^{[1]}_{N,\mathrm{e}}
gauge field,
C
is the background field for the dual 1-form symmetry (
dC=0
), whilst
G
and
l
are dynamical fields acting as a Lagrange multiplier restricting
b
to be in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
by the equations of motion. We set the theta angle in the electric frame to zero for simplicity.
25
25

25

This is sufficient to deal with duality transformations, since the self-dual coupling is purely imaginary.

We define shifted variables
b^{\prime}=b-dA
and
G^{\prime}=G+p\,dA
, so that the action reads:

S=\int\biggl[\frac{1}{2e^{2}}b^{\prime}\wedge\star\,b^{\prime}+\frac{iNp}{4\pi}b^{\prime}\wedge b^{\prime}+\frac{iN}{2\pi}b^{\prime}\wedge(C+G^{\prime})+\frac{iN}{2\pi}dA\wedge G^{\prime}-\frac{i}{2\pi}G^{\prime}\wedge dl\biggr]\;.

(3.58)

Integrating out
l
sets
G^{\prime}=dV
, and then the term
dA\wedge dV
can be dropped.
The field
b^{\prime}
can be integrated out by its equation of motion:

\frac{1}{e^{2}}\!\star b^{\prime}+\frac{iN}{2\pi}\bigl(p\,b^{\prime}+dV+C\bigr)=0\;,

(3.59)

which sets
b^{\prime}=-\frac{iN}{2\pi}\bigl(\frac{1}{e^{2}}\!\star+\frac{iNp}{2\pi}\bigr)^{-1}(dV+C)
. The action becomes

S=\int\biggl[\frac{1}{2e^{2}\beta}(dV+C)\wedge\star(dV+C)-\frac{iNp}{4\pi\beta}(dV+C)\wedge(dV+C)\biggr]

(3.60)

with
\beta=p^{2}+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{e^{4}N^{2}}
.
If we start from the self-dual coupling
\frac{1}{e^{2}}=\frac{N}{2\pi}
then the complexified coupling
\tau_{\text{C}}=\frac{\theta}{2\pi}+\frac{2\pi i}{e^{2}}
is given by

\tau_{\text{C}}^{(\mathrm{e})}=\frac{N(i-p)}{(1+p^{2})}\;.

(3.61)

This is in an electric duality frame (since
dV
couples electrically to
C
). In the magnetic frame the answer instead reads

\tau_{\text{C}}^{(\mathrm{m})}=\frac{(i+p)}{N}

(3.62)

and it reproduces the naive answer for
PSU(N)_{0}
(magnetic gauging)
\tau_{\text{C}}=i/N
. This reasoning allows us to fix the coupling
\tau_{\text{C}}
in pure Coulomb vacua with different global forms.

3.6
Cubic anomalies and invariant vacua

Lastly we perform some further consistency checks based on ’t Hooft anomalies. It is known
[
50
,
51
]
(see also
[
76
,
52
]
for a recent discussion) that the
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
group — or more appropriately its spin cover
Mp(2,\mathbb{Z})
— can have nontrivial ’t Hooft anomalies.
The ’t Hooft anomaly for a single Maxwell field,
I_{\text{Maxwell}}
, for a given cyclic subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
of the metaplectic group is related to the chiral anomaly of a Weyl fermion
I_{\text{Weyl}}
by:

I_{\text{Maxwell}}=56\,I_{\text{Weyl}}\;.

(3.63)

The latter must be carefully defined. Since
S^{4}=(-1)^{F}
, the correct structure to consider is

\text{Spin}-Mp(2,\mathbb{Z})=\frac{\text{Spin}(d)\times Mp(2,\mathbb{Z})}{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{F}}

(3.64)

where the quotient is by the diagonal
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{F}
generated by
(-1)^{F}
, and the possible anomalies are captured by a bordism group

\Omega^{\text{Spin}-Mp(2,\mathbb{Z})}_{5}(\text{pt})=\mathbb{Z}_{32}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{9}\;.

(3.65)

The first two summands stem from the cubic anomaly of
S
, while the third one from the cubic anomaly of
ST
. On the other hand, if the symmetry group were only
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{F}
, its anomalies would be classified by
[
52
]

\Omega^{\text{Spin}}_{5}\bigl(BSL(2,\mathbb{Z})\bigr)=\mathbb{Z}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{9}\;,

(3.66)

the first factor stemming from the subgroup generated by
S
while the second from
ST
.
The anomaly for the
\mathfrak{su}(N)

\mathcal{N}=4
theory can be computed on the Coulomb branch (since the scalars are not charged under S-duality and thus the symmetry is unbroken on the Coulomb branch), where we have
\text{rank}(G)=(N-1)

\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplets. Each vector multiplet contributes
56I_{\text{Weyl}}
from the vector plus
4I_{\text{Weyl}}
from the 4 Weyl fermions (which transform as if they had charge 1 under a chiral rotation, see eqn. (
2.32
))
[
51
]
. Therefore

I_{\mathcal{N}=4}=60\,(N-1)\,I_{\text{Weyl}}\;.

(3.67)

On the other hand, the chiral anomaly for
U(1)_{R}
is given by
26
26

26

The rotation
R_{-\frac{3\pi}{k}}
gives phases
e^{-\frac{3\pi i}{k}},e^{\frac{\pi i}{k}},e^{\frac{\pi i}{k}},e^{\frac{\pi i}{k}}
to the four fermions in the
\mathcal{N}=4
vector multiplet, see Table (
2.36
). Equivalently, we can use a normalization in which the charges of all fermions are integer and the periodicity of
U(1)_{R}
is
2\pi
. This amounts to assigning charges
(3,-1,-1,-1)
.

I_{\text{chiral}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{4}q_{i}^{3}\,(N^{2}-1)\,I_{\text{Weyl}}=-24\,(N^{2}-1)\,I_{\text{Weyl}}\;,

(3.68)

the minus sign stemming from the opposite action of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
and
U(1)_{R}
on the fermions. Given a cyclic subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
of
Mp(2,\mathbb{Z})
, the presence of such cubic anomalies would prevent the existence of a gapped vacuum invariant under the symmetry.
27
27

27

One might be concerned that such cubic anomalies could have a different description when the symmetry is non-invertible. On way to think about it is to work on a manifold with trivial
H^{2}(X)
, so that we are blind to the 1-form symmetry and the duality defect looks invertible. In such a case we can turn on a standard background gauge field in
H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})
.

For this to be consistent with our results, the fact that the preserved subgroup is diagonal in both
Mp(2,\mathbb{Z})
and
U(1)_{R}
is essential.

Consider first the duality generated by
S
. A crucial point is that the duality symmetry preserved in the IR starting from
\tau_{\mathrm{YM}}=i
is not
\mathbb{Z}_{8}^{F}

28
28

28

We use the superscript
F
to indicate that
(-1)^{F}
is inside the symmetry group.

but rather
\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{F}
, see the comments at the end of Section
2.3
. Thus the anomaly is valued in
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, and since
60=24=0
modulo 4, the anomaly must vanish and it does not give any constraint on the
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
physics.
29
29

29

Notice however that, were it not for the
U(1)_{R}
compensating transformation, the
\mathcal{N}=4
anomaly would be
60(N-1)=-4(N-1)\text{ mod }32
which is generically nontrivial. This happens for example in the Konishi deformation (
2.43
).

A stronger constraint comes from considering the non-invertible symmetry generated by
ST
, which is a symmetry of the theory at
\tau_{\mathrm{YM}}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}
and can be preserved by the
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
deformation using our methods.
In this case, the preserved symmetry is
\mathbb{Z}_{3}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{F}
and we must cancel the anomaly mod 9. Thus it must be that

A_{ST}=6(N-1)+3(N^{2}-1)=0\mod 9

(3.69)

whenever the theory admits a massive vacuum which preserves the triality symmetry —
i.e.
, when there is an
ST
-invariant Lagrangian lattice of dyons. We can write

A_{ST}=3N(N-1)\text{ mod }9=\begin{cases}6\text{ mod }9\;,\qquad&\text{for }N=3m+2\;,\\
0\text{ mod }9\;,\qquad&\text{otherwise}\;.\end{cases}

(3.70)

The
ST
-invariant Lagrangian lattice of dyons is present if and only if
30
30

30

This criterium for the existence of a TQFT invariant under
ST
is the analog of (
3.31
) for
S
. Its derivation is a simple generalization of the case for
S
, see
[
71
]
for a proof at the level of invariant TQFTs.

\exists\;k^{\prime},s,\tilde{\ell}\qquad\text{such that}\qquad N=(k^{\prime})^{2}s\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\ell}(\tilde{\ell}-1)+1=0\text{ mod }s\;.

(3.71)

The following table contains information about the anomaly and the presence of (possibly multiple) invariant Lagrangian lattices of dyons for small values of
N
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c | c | c}N&A_{ST}&D_{\text{invariant}}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 2&6&\text{\texttimes}\\
3&0&\langle D_{(1,1)}\rangle\\
4&0&\langle D_{(2,0)}\oplus D_{(0,2)}\rangle\\
5&6&\text{\texttimes}\\
6&0&\text{\texttimes}\\
7&0&\langle D_{(2,1)}\rangle\,,\,\langle D_{(4,1)}\rangle\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{c | c | c}N&A_{ST}&D_{\text{invariant}}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 8&6&\text{\texttimes}\\
9&0&\langle D_{(3,0)}\oplus D_{(0,3)}\rangle\\
10&0&\text{\texttimes}\\
11&6&\text{\texttimes}\\
12&0&\langle D_{(6,0)}\oplus D_{(2,2)}\rangle\\
13&0&\langle D_{(3,1)}\rangle\,,\,\langle D_{(9,1)}\rangle\end{array}

(3.72)

We indicated the generators of the lattice, when it exists.
Notice that the anomaly cancellation condition is necessary but
not sufficient
to conclude that there is an invariant vacuum, as the cases
N=6
and
N=10
show.
31
31

31

The values of
s
such that
\bigl(\ell^{2}-\ell+1=0\text{ mod }s\big)
has solutions, are those that can be represented as
s=x^{2}+xy+y^{2}
for coprime
x,y
and are
s=1,3,7,13,19,21,31,37,39,43,49,\ldots
From the representation, one sees that
s=0,1\text{ mod 3}
. Since
q^{2}=0,1\text{ mod }3
, if
N=2\text{ mod }3
then it should be
s=2\text{ mod }3
, which is not possible. This shows that anomaly cancelation is necessary to have an invariant vacuum.

Here too it is important to include the R-symmetry anomaly in order to have the required cancellation. For example, in the case
N=3
we have
24+12=0\text{ mod }9
, but neither term is vanishing on its own.

4
Gapless flows:
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=2}
orbifolds and the conifold

The existence of non-invertible duality defects is not limited to the example of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory. In this section we first construct new examples of self-duality defects in a class of
\mathcal{N}=2
Lagrangian SCFTs and then, similarly to Section
2
, we study how the defects can be preserved under deformations and what effects they have on the low-energy description.

4.1
Dualities from class
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}

We consider Lagrangian
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFTs described by a necklace quiver with
r

\mathfrak{su}(N)
nodes and bifundamental hypermultiplets. For our purposes, it is convenient to recall their class
\mathcal{S}
description which is given in terms of the 6d
\mathcal{N}=(2,0)
SCFT of type
A_{N-1}
compactified on a torus
T^{2}_{r}
with
r
distinct regular punctures. The generalized S-duality group acting on the marginal couplings is given by a quotient of the mapping class group
\mathrm{MCG}(T^{2}_{r})
of the Riemann surface
[
77
]
. For
r=1
, this is the
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
S-duality group of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM.

The group
\mathrm{MCG}(T^{2}_{r})
can be described following
[
78
]
. We denote by
\tau
the complex structure of the torus and by
p_{i}
(with
i=1,\dots,r
) the positions of the punctures. We decompose
p_{i}=x_{i}+\tau\,y_{i}
with
0<x_{i},y_{i}\leq 1
. The complexified gauge couplings of the necklace quiver are related to these parameters in the following way:

\tau_{i}=p_{i+1}-p_{i}\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq r-1\;,\qquad\tau_{r}=\tau+p_{1}-p_{r}\;,\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{r}\tau_{i}=\tau\;.

(4.1)

In order to obtain positive gauge couplings, the punctures must be ordered such that
\im(p_{i+1})>\im(p_{i})
. In this parametrization, a set of generators of
\mathrm{MCG}(T^{2}_{r})
are:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle S:

\displaystyle(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{r})\;\mapsto\;\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\tau},\,\frac{p_{1}}{\tau},\,...,\,\frac{p_{r}}{\tau}\Bigr)\,,

(4.2)

\displaystyle T:

\displaystyle(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau+1,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{r}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\ell_{i}:

\displaystyle(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{i}+1,\,...,\,p_{r}\right)\;,

\displaystyle\ell^{(\tau)}_{i}:\,

\displaystyle(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{i}+\tau,\,...,\,p_{r}\right)\;,

\displaystyle s_{i<r}:

\displaystyle(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{i+1},\,p_{i},\,...,\,p_{r}\right)\;,

\displaystyle s_{r}:

\displaystyle(\tau,\,p_{1},\,...,\,p_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau,\,p_{r}-\tau,\,p_{2},\,...,\,p_{r-1},\,p_{1}+\tau\right)\;.

In the gauge-coupling parametrization
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{r}
these transformations act as:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle S:

\displaystyle(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{r})\;\mapsto\;\Bigl(\frac{\tau_{1}}{\tau},\,...,\,\frac{\tau_{r-1}}{\tau},\,\frac{\tau_{r}-\tau-1}{\tau}\Bigr)\;,

(4.3)

\displaystyle T:

\displaystyle(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{r-1}\,,\tau_{r}+1\right)\;,

\displaystyle\ell_{i}:

\displaystyle(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{i-1}+1,\,\tau_{i}-1,\,...,\,\tau_{r}\right)\;,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\ell^{(\tau)}_{i}:\,

\displaystyle(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{i-1}+\tau,\,\tau_{i}-\tau,\,...,\,\tau_{r}\right)\;,

\displaystyle s_{i}:

\displaystyle(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{r})\;\mapsto\;\left(\tau_{1},\,...,\,\tau_{i-1}+\tau_{i},\,-\tau_{i},\,\tau_{i+1}+\tau_{i},\,...,\,\tau_{r}\right)\;.

These formulas have to be interpreted with the cyclic convention
\tau_{0}\equiv\tau_{r}
and
\tau_{r+1}\equiv\tau_{1}
. Note that these elements satisfy relations, and one could restrict to a smaller set of generators
[
78
]
. We can also introduce the element
\omega=\ell_{j}^{(\tau)}s_{j-1}s_{j-2}\dots s_{j+1}
(for any
j
) that acts as a cyclic permutation
\tau_{i}\to\tau_{i+1}
.
The case
r=2
is somewhat special. The transformations
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are inverses of each other, as are
\ell_{1}^{(\tau)}
and
\ell_{2}^{(\tau)}
. Thus we can consider only
\ell\equiv\ell_{1}
and
\ell^{(\tau)}\equiv\ell_{1}^{(\tau)}
, which act as:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\ell:

\displaystyle(\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2})\;\mapsto\;(\tau_{1}-1,\,\tau_{2}+1)\,,

(4.5)

\displaystyle\ell^{(\tau)}:\,

\displaystyle(\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2})\;\mapsto\;(\tau_{1}-\tau,\,\tau_{2}+\tau)\;.

In the same way,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are not independent and we can keep
s\equiv s_{1}
which acts by

s:\ \ (\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2})\;\mapsto\;(\tau_{2}-\tau,\,\tau_{1}+\tau)\;,

(4.6)

so that
\omega=s\,\ell^{(\tau)}
simply acts as a permutation of the two gauge couplings.

From the structure of the quiver it follows that for gauge group
SU(N)^{r}
there is a single
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry, which is the diagonal of the would-be center symmetry of each
SU(N)
factor. Accordingly, there is only a set of
N
genuine Wilson lines. Using the topological manipulation
\sigma
we can gauge the 1-form symmetry, exactly as we did for
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM based on a single node, and we end up with a theory with global form
SU(N)^{r}/\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and a dual magnetic
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry. The genuine magnetic line operators end up also carrying electric charge if one stacks with an SPT (acting with the
\tau
operation) before performing
\sigma
. Our discussion here is very brief since all details work exactly as in the single gauge group case.
The action of
S
must map a fundamental Wilson line to a fundamental ’t Hooft line. Hence
S
takes us from the global form
SU(N)^{r}
to
\bigl(SU(N)^{r}/\mathbb{Z}_{N}\bigr)_{0}
(where the suffix specifies that the ’t Hooft line does not carry electric charge), and vice versa. Similarly,
T
takes us cyclically along all the
\bigl(SU(N)^{r}/\mathbb{Z}_{N}\bigr)_{k}
global forms for
k=0,\dots,N-1
.

One should be careful with the physical interpretation of
\mathrm{MCG}(T^{2}_{r})
. Its elements in general do not preserve the ordering of the punctures,
i.e.
, they do not preserve the fact that
\im\tau_{i}>0
. Such elements do not map a physical point on the conformal manifold to another one, and thus do not qualify as elements of the S-duality group. However, it is always possible to combine those elements with a suitable string of permutations
s_{i}
and shifts
\ell_{i}^{(\tau)}
such that all
\im\tau_{i}>0
after the transformation.
32
32

32

In the gauge coupling parametrization, the permutations
s_{i}
correspond to a change in the sign of
\tau_{i}
. In the type IIA construction of
[
77
]
they are interpreted as an exchange of two adjacent NS5 branes on
S^{1}
(modulo cyclic permutations).

These, and only these, are the elements of the S-duality group.
33
33

33

The particular string to use depends on the point
\{\tau_{i}\}
we start with, not just on the element we consider. Thus the S-duality group is a quotient of
\mathrm{MCG}(T^{2}_{r})
.

We then see that only the generators
S
and
T
act on the global form, in the very same way as in
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

We can now identify the fixed points of S-duality.

Example:
\boldsymbol{r=2}
.

This is the
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT consisting of two
\mathfrak{su}(N)
gauge nodes. It corresponds to a torus
T^{2}_{2}
with two punctures. Since
S
and
T
act in the standard way on
\tau
, we fix it at the
S
duality invariant point
\tau=i
. The action of
S
duality can be geometrically interpreted as a 90-degree clockwise rotation around the bottom-left corner of the torus. The invariant position of the punctures, up to a shift, is

p_{1}=\frac{i}{2}\;,\qquad p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}+i\;,\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\tau_{1}=\frac{i+1}{2}\;,\qquad\tau_{2}=\frac{i-1}{2}\;.

(4.7)

Indeed, the transformation that leaves them invariant is as shown in the picture below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{379.35356pt}{62.38295pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 492.13 39.37 M 494.09 39.37 C 494.09 40.46 493.21 41.34 492.13 41.34 C 491.04 41.34 490.16 40.46 490.16 39.37 C 490.16 38.28 491.04 37.4 492.13 37.4 C 493.21 37.4 494.09 38.28 494.09 39.37 Z M 492.13 39.37}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{342.60912pt}{27.2722pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(4.8)

At each step we have dressed the transformations in such a way that the gauge couplings are positive.
Following the discussion in Section
2
, we construct a topological interface
I_{\Sigma}
that acts on the marginal couplings of the
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT as follows:

\Sigma\,\equiv\,\ell_{2}^{(\tau)}s_{1}\ell_{2}^{-1}\ell_{2}^{(\tau)}S=\omega\,\ell\,\ell^{(\tau)-1}S=s\,\ell\,S\;.

(4.9)

Composing such topological interface with another topological interface
\varphi_{\sigma}
that implements the topological manipulation
\sigma
, we define a topological operator
\mathfrak{D}_{\Sigma}=\varphi^{\dagger}_{\sigma}\circ I_{\Sigma}
that implements the non-invertible self-duality symmetry of the theory. A similar reasoning applies to the triality defects at
\tau=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}
.
As we will see, the defect
\mathfrak{D}_{\Sigma}
can be preserved under a massive
\mathcal{N}=1
RG flow.

Example:
\boldsymbol{r=4}
.

The previous discussion generalizes to the case of
T_{4}^{2}
,
i.e.
, an
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT with a necklace quiver with four
\mathfrak{su}(N)
gauge nodes. In this case there is a one-dimensional complex line of points within the conformal manifold which are fixed under
S
duality, given by
\tau=i
and

(p_{1},\,p_{2},\,p_{3},\,p_{4})=\bigl(\alpha,\;1+i\alpha,\ i-i\alpha,\;1+i-\alpha\bigr)\quad\text{with}\quad 0<\im(\alpha)<\re(\alpha)<\frac{1}{2}\;.

(4.10)

Using (
4.1
), this configuration corresponds to the following complexified gauge couplings:

(\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2},\,\tau_{3},\,\tau_{4})=\bigl(1+(i-1)\alpha,\;i-1-2i\alpha,\;1+(i-1)\alpha,\;-1+2\alpha\bigr)\;.

(4.11)

The gauge couplings are all positive. We act on the punctures with (
4.2
) in such a way to leave them invariant:

\bigl(\alpha,\;1+i\alpha,\;i-i\alpha,\;i+1-\alpha\bigr)\;\xrightarrow{S}\;\bigl(-i\alpha,\;-i+\alpha,\;1-\alpha,\;1-i+i\alpha\bigr)\;\to\\
\xrightarrow{\prod_{i=1}^{4}\ell_{i}^{(\tau)}}\;\bigl(i-i\alpha,\;\alpha,\;1+i-\alpha,\;1+i\alpha\bigr)\;\xrightarrow{\pi}\;\bigl(\alpha,\;1+i\alpha,\;i-i\alpha,\;i+1-\alpha\bigr)\;,

(4.12)

where
\pi=s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}
. We visualize these transformations in the picture below:

{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 417.32 68.9 M 419.29 68.9 C 419.29 69.98 418.41 70.87 417.32 70.87 C 416.24 70.87 415.35 69.98 415.35 68.9 C 415.35 67.81 416.24 66.93 417.32 66.93 C 418.41 66.93 419.29 67.81 419.29 68.9 Z M 417.32 68.9}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{288.54906pt}{48.61177pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(4.13)

Once again we construct a topological interface
I_{\Sigma}
, where
\Sigma=\pi\prod_{i}\ell_{i}^{(\tau)}S
, which we compose with a topological interface that implements the action of
\sigma
in order to construct a non-invertible self-duality symmetry defect
\mathfrak{D}_{\Sigma}
.

We conclude this section with some extra comments on higher values of
r
. For
r=4k
and
r=4k+2
the story is similar, as we can always organize the punctures into
k
squares as in
r=4
, possibly plus two more punctures as in
r=2
. Note that all punctures must be distinct. For odd values of
r=2n+1
we can place
2n
punctures as in the previous case of
r
even, plus one puncture in the middle of the torus,
e.g.
:

\displaystyle\hbox to95.36pt{\vbox to86.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
