(4.14)

Using the same techniques we find, for example if
r=5
:
\Sigma=s_{5}\,s_{4}\,s_{1}\prod_{i=1}^{5}\ell_{i}^{(\tau)}\,S
.

4.2
Non-invertible duality defects on the conifold

Starting from the
\mathcal{N}=2
quiver SCFTs and their self-duality defects introduced above, we can adopt the same strategy as in Section
2
and introduce relevant deformations that partially break supersymmetry to
\mathcal{N}=1
, while preserving the non-invertible symmetry. A particularly interesting example is the
r=2
theory considered above,
i.e.
, the
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT consisting of two
\mathfrak{su}(N)
gauge nodes and two bifundamental hypermultiplets. We decompose the hypermultiplets in terms of
\mathcal{N}=1
chiral superfields
A_{\alpha}
,
B_{\beta}
with
\alpha,\beta=1,2
transforming respectively in the
(N,\overline{N})
and
(\overline{N},N)
representation, while we call
\Phi
and
\tilde{\Phi}
the adjoint chiral multiplets in the
\mathcal{N}=2
vector multiplets. The superpotential is

W=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\Tr\bigl(\Phi A_{\alpha}B_{\beta}+\tilde{\Phi}B_{\alpha}A_{\beta}\bigr)=\Tr\Phi(A_{1}B_{2}-A_{2}B_{1})+\Tr\tilde{\Phi}(B_{1}A_{2}-B_{2}A_{1})\;.

(4.15)

With two gauge nodes there is a non-Abelian
SU(2)
flavor symmetry under which
A_{\alpha}
and
B_{\beta}
transform as doublets.

We can show that there exist massive deformations of the
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT that preserve the non-invertible symmetry defect
\mathfrak{D}_{\Sigma}
. Consider the superpotential deformation

\delta W=\frac{m}{2}\,\bigl(\Tr\Phi^{2}-\Tr\tilde{\Phi}^{2}\bigr)

(4.16)

that partially breaks supersymmetry to
\mathcal{N}=1
. Any
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT has
SU(2)_{R}\times U(1)_{\mathsf{r}}
R-symmetry group. The supercharges
Q_{\alpha}^{i}
(
i=1,2
) transform as doublets of
SU(2)_{R}
and with charge
[Q^{i}_{\alpha}]_{\mathsf{r}}=-1
under
U(1)_{\mathsf{r}}
. In our conventions the superspace coordinate measure
d^{2}\theta
transforms with charge
[d^{2}\theta]_{\mathsf{r}}=-1
. Both
\Tr\Phi^{2}
and
\Tr\tilde{\Phi}^{2}
are neutral under
SU(2)_{R}
and have
U(1)_{\mathsf{r}}
charge
[\Tr\Phi^{2}]_{\mathsf{r}}=[\Tr\tilde{\Phi}^{2}]_{\mathsf{r}}=2
. As a result,
\int\!d^{2}\theta\,\delta W
has charge
1
.
Under a
\Sigma
-duality transformation, the integrated superpotential deformation
\int\!d^{2}\theta\,\delta W
picks up a phase
-e^{-\frac{i\pi}{2}}
(notice the sign difference with respect to our discussion in Section
2.3
). This is due to the fact that
\Sigma
involves the action of
\omega
that sends
\Phi\to\tilde{\Phi}
and
m\to-m
.
Since the mass deformation (
4.16
) breaks the superconformal
U(1)_{\mathsf{r}}
explicitly, we can use a
U(1)_{\mathsf{r}}
transformation to compensate for the action of
\Sigma
:

\mathfrak{D}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}=\mathsf{r}_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}\times\mathfrak{D}_{\Sigma}

(4.17)

is a symmetry of the deformed theory.
The effect of (
4.16
) can be understood more clearly upon integrating out the adjoint chiral fields
\Phi
and
\tilde{\Phi}

[
57
]
. This leads to an
\mathcal{N}=1
theory with
\mathfrak{su}(N)\times\mathfrak{su}(N)
gauge group, bifundamental chiral fields
A_{\alpha}
,
B_{\dot{\alpha}}
(
\alpha,\dot{\alpha}=1,2
) and a quartic superpotential

W_{\mathrm{KW}}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\,\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\,\Tr A_{\alpha}B_{\dot{\alpha}}A_{\beta}B_{\dot{\beta}}=\lambda\Tr\bigl(A_{1}B_{1}A_{2}B_{2}-A_{1}B_{2}A_{2}B_{1}\bigr)\;,

(4.18)

where
\lambda=-1/m
, exhibiting an enhanced
SU(2)\times SU(2)
global symmetry. The choice of relative sign in (
4.16
) is crucial to obtain such enhancement. There is another choice of mass deformation that preserves the non-invertible symmetry:
\delta W=\frac{m}{2}\bigl(\Tr\Phi^{2}+\Tr\tilde{\Phi}^{2}\bigr)
.

Using the methods of
[
66
]
one can show that the
\mathcal{N}=1
theory flows to a non-trivial IR fixed point, where the dimension of the chiral superfields is
3/4
(as determined from their
R
-charge
1/2
). The operator (
4.18
) has
R
-charge
2
and is an exaclty marginal deformation of the
\mathcal{N}=1
SCFT which, together with the gauge couplings, gives rise to manifold of fixed points of complex dimension 2. We refer to this theory, introduced in
[
57
]
to describe a stack of
N
D3-branes probing a conifold singularity, as the Klebanov-Witten (KW) theory or as the conifold theory.

As opposed to the case of the
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
theory discussed in Section
3
, the adjoint mass deformation (
4.16
) leads to an interacting gapless IR theory with no spontaneous breaking of the non-invertible symmetry. We should then understand how S-duality acts on the conformal manifold of the conifold theory. To address this, we will first resort to AdS/CFT, and then perform a purely field theoretic analysis.

For general
\mathbb{Z}_{r}
orbifold SCFTs, we can also turn on adjoint mass deformations

\delta W=\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{r}\,m_{i}\Tr(\Phi_{i}^{2})

(4.19)

that are preserved by the duality
\Sigma
up to a phase (then reabsorbed by an R-symmetry rotation). For small masses, the
\mathcal{N}=2
duality group acts as
[
78
]
:

\displaystyle S:

\displaystyle(m,\,m_{1},\,...,\,m_{r})\;\mapsto\;\Bigl(\frac{m}{\tau},\,m_{1}-m\frac{\tau_{1}}{\tau},\,...,\,m_{r-1}-m\frac{\tau_{r-1}}{\tau},\,m_{r}-m\frac{\tau_{r}-1}{\tau}\Bigr)\,,

(4.20)

\displaystyle\ell_{i}^{(\tau)}:\,

\displaystyle(m,\,m_{1},\,...,\,m_{r})\;\mapsto\;(m,m_{1},\,...,\,m_{i-1}+m,\,m_{i}-m,\,...,\,m_{r}),\,

\displaystyle s_{i}:

\displaystyle(m,\,m_{1},\,...,\,m_{r})\;\mapsto\;(m,m_{1},\,...,\,m_{i-1}+m_{i},\,-m_{i},\,m_{i+1}+m_{i},\,...,\,m_{r})\;.

We introduced
m=\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}
, and in the last two formulas one has to use the cyclic convention
m_{0}\equiv m_{r}
and
m_{r+1}\equiv m_{1}
. We then look for mass configurations that at
\tau=i
are invariant under
\Sigma
up to a phase. For
r=2
(at
\tau_{1}=\frac{i+1}{2}
) one finds two solutions for
m_{1},m_{2}
:

m_{2}=-m_{1}:\;(m_{1},m_{2})\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{\mapsto}}\,-(m_{1},m_{2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad m_{2}=m_{1}:\;(m_{1},m_{2})\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma}}{{\mapsto}}\,-i\,(m_{1},m_{2})\;.

(4.21)

This reproduces what we discussed above. For
r=4
we find that
m_{1}=-m_{3}=m
and
m_{2}=m_{4}=0
gives a consistent duality-preserving deformation (but there might be more). It would be interesting to study this problem further.

4.3
Holographic aspects

Both the
\mathcal{N}=2
quiver SCFT with
r=2
nodes and the
\mathcal{N}=1
KW SCFT are paradigmatic examples of AdS/CFT. They arise from D3-branes probing toric Calabi-Yau singularities, here
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{C}
and the conifold, respectively. These two singularities have a single vanishing 2-cycle, where D5-branes can be wrapped yielding fractional branes. A regular D3-brane, when placed at the singularity, breaks into two types of mutually BPS states, each corresponding to one node of the quiver. As long as the NSNS 2-form gauge potential
B_{2}
is in a certain range — (
4.23
) below — the two states are a D5-brane wrapped on the 2-cycle, and an anti-D5-brane on the 2-cycle with
-1
units of worldvolume flux
[
79
,
80
,
81
]
. If the number of D5 and
\overline{\text{D5}}
is the same, the two nodes have the same rank and the quiver gauge theory is a SCFT, while if it is not the same, then an interesting RG flow occurs, where the ranks of the gauge groups gradually diminish towards the IR
[
82
,
83
,
84
]
. Here we will be interested in the conformal case.

Both theories above have a (symmetry preserving) conformal manifold that can be parameterized by two complex couplings. In the
\mathcal{N}=2
case these are simply the two gauge couplings, while in the
\mathcal{N}=1
KW theory the conformal manifold is embedded in the space of the two gauge couplings and the quartic superpotential coupling. In the latter case the two complex parameters are not exactly identified with the
\mathcal{N}=1
gauge couplings, although, following
[
85
]
, we still assume that they have a positive imaginary part. In both SCFTs there is a simple way to determine the holographic description of the exactly marginal couplings via probe branes.

Let us first consider the
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT. By looking at the DBI + WZ action of a D3-brane at the
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{C}
singularity, and at a D5-brane wrapped on the vanishing 2-cycle, one obtains the following map between field theory and supergravity parameters:

\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}=\frac{\tau_{\text{SG}}}{g_{s}}\equiv\frac{1}{g_{s}}\,\bigl(C_{0}+i\,e^{-\phi}\bigr)\;,\qquad\quad\tau_{1}=-\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}g_{s}}\int_{S^{2}}\bigl(C_{2}-\tau_{\text{SG}}\,B_{2}\bigr)\;,

(4.22)

where
\tau_{j}=\theta_{j}/2\pi+4\pi i/g_{j}^{2}
are the
\mathcal{N}=2
complexified gauge couplings,
C_{0}
and
C_{2}
are the RR 0-form and 2-form fields of type IIB supergravity,
\phi
is the dilaton, and
B_{2}
the NSNS 2-form field. Given that
\im\tau_{j}
should be positive, these formulas are correct only if

0<\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{S^{2}}B_{2}<1\;.

(4.23)

Indeed, only in this range a wrapped D5 and a wrapped anti-D5 with
-1
units of worldvolume flux have positive D3 charge and can be mutually BPS. Outside of the range the decomposition is different: for
n<\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}}\!\int\!B_{2}<n+1
the mutually BPS states are a wrapped D5 with
-n
units of flux, and a wrapped anti-D5 with
-n-1
units of flux. The formulas (
4.22
) for the gauge couplings should be accordingly modified. We will take the alternative point of view that one should first perform a large gauge transformation of
B_{2}
, corresponding to the integer shift
\delta\,\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{S^{2}}B_{2}=-n
in order to map it inside the range (
4.23
), and then apply (
4.22
).

The relevant type IIB
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
transformations are the following:

\tau_{\text{SG}}\;\mapsto\;\frac{a\,\tau_{\text{SG}}+b}{c\,\tau_{\text{SG}}+d}\;,\qquad\qquad\biggl(\begin{matrix}C_{2}\\
B_{2}\end{matrix}\biggr)\;\mapsto\;\biggl(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\biggr)\biggl(\begin{matrix}C_{2}\\
B_{2}\end{matrix}\biggr)\;,

(4.24)

under which we have

C_{2}-\tau_{\text{SG}}\,B_{2}\;\mapsto\;\frac{1}{c\,\tau_{\text{SG}}+d}\,\bigl(C_{2}-\tau_{\text{SG}}\,B_{2}\bigr)\;.

(4.25)

We can then deduce the
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
transformations of the complexified gauge couplings. We set
g_{s}=1
and identify
\tau=\tau_{\text{SG}}=\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
. Naively, we obtain the transformations:

\tau\;\mapsto\;\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\;,\qquad\qquad\tau_{1}\;\mapsto\;\frac{\tau_{1}}{c\tau+d}\;,

(4.26)

and
\tau_{2}=\tau-\tau_{1}
. They agree with the transformations in (
4.2
).
34
34

34

To see this more clearly, one should use the gauge coupling basis
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
introduced in (
4.3
) which is more natural for the comparison with supergravity.

As discussed in Section
4.1
, one should be careful since these transformations do not always respect
\im\tau_{j}>0
. In fact, from the point of view of supergravity we also have large gauge transformations of
B_{2}
and
C_{2}
which shift their respective integrals by integer amounts.
These give rise to transformations
\ell^{(\tau)}
and
\ell
acting as in (
4.5
).
The transformation
\ell
has a clear field-theoretical interpretation as a shift of the theta angles. The transformation
\ell^{(\tau)}
is more subtle: since
\im\tau>0
,
\ell^{(\tau)}
is such that in its orbit there is one and only one representative with
\im\tau_{1,2}>0
. Therefore the transformation in (
4.26
) has to be combined with a suitable repetition of
\ell^{(\tau)}
(whose order depends on
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
) in (
4.5
) in order to respect
\im\tau_{j}>0
. We find:

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=-\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)\;,\quad

\displaystyle\ell^{(\tau)}C:\quad\tau

\displaystyle\to\tau\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{2}\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{1}

(4.27)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)\;,\quad

\displaystyle T:\quad\tau

\displaystyle\to\tau+1\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{1}\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{2}+1

\displaystyle\ell T:\quad\tau

\displaystyle\to\tau+1\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{1}+1\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\to\tau_{2}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)\;,\quad

\displaystyle\left[\ell^{(\tau)}\right]^{n}S:\quad\tau

\displaystyle\to-\frac{1}{\tau}\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\tau_{1}}{\tau}+n\tau\;,\quad

\displaystyle\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\to-\frac{\tau_{1}+1}{\tau}-n\tau\;.

In the last line the integer power
n
is a function of
\tau
and
\tau_{1}
. Notice that
C
is the holographic avatar of
s
from (
4.6
).

One can repeat the holographic analysis verbatim for the
\mathcal{N}=1
KW SCFT by studying a D3-brane probing the conifold singularity and a D5-brane wrapped on the vanishing 2-cycle. In this context we continue using the notation
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
to parametrize the conformal manifold although, as emphasized before, in the Klebanov-Witten model
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are not simply related to the gauge couplings. The holographic description indicates that the
\mathcal{N}=2
generalized
S
-duality transformations are inherited by the Klebanov-Witten theory. This is what we expected from the analysis of Section
4.2
.

4.4
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=1}
non-invertible symmetry from Seiberg duality

As shown in Section
4.2
, the Klebanov-Witten theory has a non-invertible self-duality symmetry inherited from its parent
\mathcal{N}=2
SCFT. Further evidence of this symmetry comes from the holographic realization of the theory, as discussed in Section
4.3
. It is natural to ask whether it is possible to describe the S-duality symmetry in purely
\mathcal{N}=1
field theoretic terms. It turns out that, at least for
N=2
, one can understand S-duality of the KW theory in terms of Seiberg duality. The argument does not apply to larger values of
N
, and it would be worth exploring this question further.

In the specific case of
N=2
, the
\mathfrak{su}(2)\times\mathfrak{su}(2)
conifold theory can be described as an
\mathfrak{so}(4)
theory with
N_{f}=4
flavors in the fundamental representation. The global variant
Spin(4)
corresponds to
SU(2)\times SU(2)
, while
SO(4)_{\pm}
are identified with
P\bigl(SU(2)\times SU(2)\bigr){}_{0,1}
.
35
35

35

By
P\bigl(SU(2)\times SU(2)\bigr)
we mean
\bigl(SU(2)\times SU(2)\bigr)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
where the quotient is by the diagonal center. See
[
38
]
for an in-depth treatment of
\mathfrak{so}
theories with matter in the vector representation.

The
Spin(4)
theory with fundamental matter has a
\mathbb{Z}^{[1]}_{2}
electric 1-form symmetry acting on the spinor Wilson line that we denote by
s
, while the Wilson line in the vector representation
V
is screened. In
SU(2)^{2}
language, the line
s
corresponds to a fundamental Wilson line
W_{1}
for the first gauge group, or
W_{2}
for the second gauge group, while the Wilson line
V=W_{1}W_{2}
can be screened by the bifundamental matter.
The
SO(4)_{+}
variant has an ’t Hooft line
T
which is charged under the dual magnetic
1
-form symmetry, while the
SO(4)_{-}
variant has a genuine dyonic line denoted by
sT
.
We denote the chiral multiplets by
v^{i}_{\alpha}
where
\alpha
is an index in the fundamental representation of the flavor symmetry group
SU(4)
, while
i
is in the vector representation of the gauge algebra
\mathfrak{so}(4)
. The Klebanov-Witten superpotential can be written as

W_{\text{KW}}=\frac{1}{2}\,\lambda\,\epsilon_{ijkl}\,\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\,v^{i}_{\alpha}\,v^{j}_{\beta}\,v^{k}_{\gamma}\,v^{l}_{\delta}=\lambda\,B\;,

(4.28)

where
B
is the baryon operator. The action of Seiberg duality on
\mathfrak{so}(N)
gauge theories with
N_{f}\geq N\geq 4
and vanishing superpotential has been studied in
[
38
]
and it displays a nontrivial mapping between global variants:

\displaystyle Spin(N)+N_{f}\

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\quad\;SO(N_{f}-N+4)_{-}+N_{f}

(4.29)

\displaystyle SO(N)_{+}+N_{f}\

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\quad\;SO(N_{f}-N+4)_{+}+N_{f}\,.

Notice that this duality action is
different
from the one of
S
duality, which maps
Spin
to
SO_{+}
since it maps a Wilson line to an ’t Hooft line.

The case of
N_{f}=N=4
is doubly special. First, the gauge algebras are self-dual since
N_{f}-N-4=N=4
. Second, for
N_{f}=N
we should consider the baryon operator
B
. Its Seiberg dual is the difference of the gauge kinetic terms for the two
\mathfrak{su}(2)
factors
[
86
]
:

B\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\Tr\bigl(\mathcal{W}_{(1)}^{2}-\mathcal{W}_{(2)}^{2}\bigr)\;,

(4.30)

and thus the dual of the KW superpotential is a shift of the relative coupling
\eta=\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}
and vice versa.
36
36

36

In this section we denote by
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
the
\mathcal{N}=1
gauge couplings, which should not be confused with the exactly marginal gauge couplings parametrizing the two-dimensional conformal manifold that we discussed in Section
4.3
. We hope this does not cause confusion to the reader.

It is convenient to parametrize a point on the
SU(4)
-preserving conformal manifold by
(\eta,\lambda)
, so that the total gauge coupling
\tau=\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
is a specific function
\tau(\eta,\lambda)
whose precise form is not known. We denote a KW theory by the data
\bigl(G+N_{f},\,\eta,\,W\bigr)
where
G
is the global form of the gauge group. We then use the results of
[
38
]
to build a self-duality. Starting from
\bigl(Spin(4)+4,\,\eta,\,\lambda B)
we perform a first Seiberg duality to
\bigl(SO(4)_{-}+4,\,\lambda^{\prime},\,\eta^{\prime}B^{\prime}+Mv^{\prime}v^{\prime})
, where the primed variables
\eta^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}
are some functions of the unprimed ones,
M
is the meson field, and
v^{\prime}
are the dual quark fields. Then we perform a shift of
\theta_{1}
by
2\pi
, which also maps the
SO(4)_{-}
theory to
SO(4)_{+}
. The relative gauge coupling
\eta
is shifted by 1, and so is
\tau
. This induces a nontrivial map
\lambda\to\tilde{\lambda}
on the baryon coupling, such that

\tau\bigl(\eta+1,\,\tilde{\lambda}\bigr)=\tau(\eta,\lambda)+1\;.

(4.31)

Finally, we perform a second Seiberg duality from
SO(4)_{+}
to
SO(4)_{+}
and then integrate out the new meson field
M^{\prime}
through its equations of motion. In this way we obtain a dual description in terms of
\bigl(SO(4)_{+}\,+\,4,\;\eta^{\prime\prime},\;\lambda^{\prime\prime}\bigr)
. We interpret this duality as the
\mathcal{N}=1
version in the conifold of the
\mathcal{N}=2
S-duality element
\Sigma
, and we denote it as
\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}=1}
. Importantly, one can derive that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{N}=1}
maps the global form
Spin(4)
to
SO(4)_{+}
.

In order to define a self-duality defect we must assume that there exists a fixed point
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime\prime}
,
\eta=\eta^{\prime\prime}
, and then compose the map with a discrete gauging of the
\mathbb{Z}^{[1]}_{2}
symmetry. We propose that this gives rise to a non-invertible self-duality symmetry defect.
37
37

37

This duality is absent in the theory without superpotential, since Seiberg duality generates a Klebanov-Witten-type term
[
38
]
.

We summarize the construction in the following graph:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-120.38138pt}{-6.62001pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{

Appendix A
TQFTs for
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=1}
SYM

In this appendix we briefly review the TQFT description of the vacua of pure
\mathcal{N}=1

\mathfrak{su}(N)
SYM. Let us start with
G=SU(N)
with its
N
equivalent confining vacua. In the
k
-th vacuum the dyon
D_{(k,1)}
condenses and the theory is described by
38
38

38

Here, for simplicity, we turn off the gauge field for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2N}
R-symmetry. That symmetry has a cubic remnant of the chiral anomaly, which is saturated in the IR and it becomes an ’t Hooft anomaly for the domain wall operators.

\text{SPT}_{-k}(B)=\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi ik}{2N}\int\mathfrak{P}(B)\right)\;.

(A.1)

These vacua form
N
“universes”, which can be described by the TQFT of a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-valued scalar
\phi
with the following partition function:

Z(B)=\sum_{\phi\,\in H^{0}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})}\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i}{2N}\int\phi\cup\mathfrak{P}(B)\biggr)\;.

(A.2)

This TQFT reproduces the mixed anomaly

I=\frac{2\pi i(N-1)}{2N}\int A\cup\mathfrak{P}(B)

(A.3)

between the
\mathbb{Z}_{2N}
R-symmetry and the 1-form symmetry. Denoting as
V_{n}=e^{in\phi}
the point-like operators, we can construct the projector
P_{k}
on the
k
-th vacuum as

P_{k}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}nk}\;V_{n}\;.

(A.4)

It is simple to show that the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
0-form symmetry defects
U_{n}
satisfy

U_{n}\;P_{k}=P_{k+n}\;U_{n}\;.

(A.5)

These are the topological avatars of
\mathcal{N}=1
domain walls between confining vacua. In order to be consistent with the action (
A.2
), the domain wall must also be stacked with an
\text{SPT}_{1}(B)
theory when changing vacuum. This works as an inflow theory for a minimal
\mathcal{A}^{N,1}
TQFT, thus

\text{DW}=U\times\mathcal{A}^{N,1}(B)\;,

(A.6)

which is indeed a subsector of the Acharya-Vafa theory
[
87
]
.

In the
PSU(N)_{0}
case, we can find the TQFT of vacua by gauging the 1-form symmetry:

Z(C)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\rvert}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\,\in H^{0}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\\
b\,\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\end{subarray}}\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i}{2N}\int\phi\cup\mathfrak{P}(b)+\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int b\cup C\biggr)\;.

(A.7)

The vacua are now generically inequivalent and host a
\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,N)}
TQFT. Following
[
15
]
, the IR domain wall becomes a non-invertible defect
\mathfrak{D}=U\times\mathcal{A}^{N,1}(b)
.

Appendix B
Local order parameters for
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=1^{*}}

In this appendix we recall the transformation properties of the order parameter introduced in Section
3.1
, following the discussion in
[
88
]
. Neglecting some prefactors, we can rewrite the superpotential (
3.7
) in terms of the following functions:

\displaystyle h^{(N)}(\tau)

\displaystyle=E_{2}(\tau)-NE_{2}(N\tau)\;,

(B.1)

\displaystyle g^{(k)}_{\ell}(\tau)

\displaystyle=E_{2}(\tau)-\frac{k^{\prime}}{k}E_{2}\biggl(\frac{k^{\prime}\tau+\ell}{k}\biggr)\;,\quad N=kk^{\prime}\;,\quad\ell=0,\ldots,k-1\;,

where
h^{(N)}(\tau)
corresponds to the (unique) fully Higgsed vacuum.

The Eisenstein series
E_{2}(\tau)
, that we can evaluate as

E_{2}(\tau)=\frac{12}{\pi i}\,\frac{\eta^{\prime}(\tau)}{\eta(\tau)}

(B.2)

in terms of the Dedekind eta function
\eta(\tau)
, is a quasi-modular form of degree 2, therefore

E_{2}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=(c\tau+d)^{2}E_{2}(\tau)+\frac{6c(c\tau+d)}{\pi i}\;.

(B.3)

An immediate consequence is that
h^{(N)}(\tau+1)=h^{(N)}(\tau)
and
g^{(k)}_{\ell}(\tau+1)=g^{(k)}_{\ell+k^{\prime}}(\tau)
, consistently with the expected properties of these vacua under the modular
T
transformation. In addtion, from (
B.3
) it is straightforward to obtain

h^{(N)}\biggl(-\frac{1}{\tau}\biggr)=\tau^{2}\,g_{0}^{(N)}(\tau)\;,\qquad\qquad g^{(N)}_{0}\biggl(-\frac{1}{\tau}\biggr)=\tau^{2}\,h^{(N)}(\tau)\;.

(B.4)

Now let us consider an integer
\ell>0
, with
\gcd(\ell,N)=p
. Define
\tilde{p}=-\bigl(\ell/p\bigr)^{-1}_{\text{mod }N/p}
in other words there exists an integer
r
such that
-\tilde{p}\,\ell/p-rN/p=1
. The the following holds:

\frac{-\frac{1}{\tau}+\ell}{N}=M\cdot\tau^{\prime}\qquad\text{with}\qquad M=\begin{pmatrix}\ell/p&r\\
N/p&-\tilde{p}\end{pmatrix}\;,\qquad\tau^{\prime}=\frac{p\,\tau+\tilde{p}}{N/p}\;,

(B.5)

where
M\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
acts by fractional linear transformations.
Combining this with the transformation (
B.3
) yields

g^{(N)}_{\ell}\biggl(-\frac{1}{\tau}\biggr)=\tau^{2}\,g^{(N/p)}_{\tilde{p}}(\tau)\;.

(B.6)

When
N
is prime, the transformations (
B.4
) and (
B.6
) (with
p=1
) account for the mapping of the order parameter under the modular
S
transformation for all the vacua.

For
N=kk^{\prime}
the procedure is similar. Denoting
\gcd(\ell,k)=p
and
\tilde{p}=-\bigl(\ell/p\bigr)^{-1}_{\text{mod }k/p}
so that there exists
r
such that
-\tilde{p}\,\ell/p-rk/p=1
, we note that

\frac{-\frac{k^{\prime}}{\tau}+\ell}{k}=\hat{M}\cdot\hat{\tau}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\hat{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\ell/p&r\\
k/p&-\tilde{p}\end{pmatrix}\;,\qquad\hat{\tau}=\frac{\frac{p\tau}{k^{\prime}}+\tilde{p}}{k/p}\;.

(B.7)

This relation, together with (
B.3
), leads to

g^{(k)}_{\ell}\biggl(-\frac{1}{\tau}\biggr)=\tau^{2}\,g^{(N/p)}_{k^{\prime}\tilde{p}}(\tau)\;.

(B.8)

The expression above perfectly matches the action of
S
-duality on the Lagrangian sublattices (
2.10
), according to the correspondence between gapped vacua in
SU(N)

\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
SYM and global forms for the algebra
\mathfrak{su}(N)
.

When
N
is a perfect square,
i.e.
, when
k^{\prime}=k
, there is a vacuum for which the order parameter vanishes identically for any value of
\tau
, namely

g^{(k)}_{0}(\tau)=0\qquad\forall\tau\;.

(B.9)

This corresponds to the presence of a vacuum state which is invariant under the full
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, as we illustrate in the example of
N=4
in the main text.

Appendix C
TQFTs and topological manipulations

Here we provide some details about the topological field theories introduced in the main text as well as the non-trivial operations performed on them.
Most of the calculations described below rely on the ability to decompose a given cocycle in terms of elements belonging to different cohomologies. Moreover, it is crucial for the consistency of the computations that the resulting expressions are themselves unrestricted sums over such cohomologies. However, this is not generally the case if one allows for an arbitrary spacetime topology. We then proceed to describe under which conditions these manipulations can be performed.

Consider a dynamical 2-form field
b\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})
, where
X
denotes the spacetime manifold. Take
N=kk^{\prime}
and perform the decomposition
b=k\,c+b^{\prime}
. Naively, one would say that
c
and
b^{\prime}
are arbitrary elements in
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})
and
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})
, respectively. Instead, these two bundles are correlated by the fact that
db
must be trivial. To properly define this procedure consider the group extension

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{k}\longrightarrow 1\;.

(C.1)

This short exact sequence splits if and only if
\gcd(k,k^{\prime})=1
. If
\gcd(k,k^{\prime})\neq 1
, then consistency with the short exact sequence implies the following correlation between the bundles

dc=-\beta(b^{\prime})\;,\qquad\qquad\text{where}\qquad\beta:H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})\to H^{3}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})

(C.2)

is the Bockstein map. We could think of
\beta(b^{\prime})\equiv b^{\prime\,*}e
as the pull-back of an extension class
e\in H^{3}(B^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{k},\,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})

[
74
]
characterizing the bundle.
Therefore,
b^{\prime}
is not an arbitrary element of
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})
, rather it has to be an element such that
\beta(b^{\prime})
is trivial in cohomology. In other words,
b^{\prime}
is restricted to the cocycles that trivialize the extension class
e
under the pull-back
b^{\prime\,*}e
. This would imply that
b^{\prime}
is not freely summed over, hence making it difficult to integrate it out.

In order to highlight how this obstacle may be avoided, let us uplift
b^{\prime}
to a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued cochain, such that the Bockstein map reads

\beta(b^{\prime})=\frac{1}{k}\,db^{\prime}\;.

(C.3)

In order to understand if
\beta(b^{\prime})
can be non-trivial in cohomology, we integrate it on a 3-cycle
\gamma_{3}
. If
\partial\gamma_{3}=0
, by Stokes theorem we immediately find that the integral vanishes. However, if
H_{2}(X)
has torsion, we could have
\partial\gamma_{3}=n\gamma_{2}
so that
\int_{\gamma_{3}}\beta(b^{\prime})=\frac{n}{k}\int_{\gamma_{2}}b^{\prime}\neq 0
. Thus, if
H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is freely generated, this cannot happen and
\beta(b^{\prime})
is necessarily trivial in cohomology, meaning that (
C.2
) does not impose any constraint on
b^{\prime}
. We will restrict to simply-connected four-manifolds, so as to guarantee that
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is freely generated.
39
39

39

A simply-connected manifold has
H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Z})=0
. The universal coefficient theorem for cohomology states that there exists a short exact sequence
0\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(H_{i-1}(X;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z}\bigr)\to H^{i}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(H_{i}(X;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z}\bigr)\to 0
. Taking
i=2
, on a simply-connected manifold
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigl(H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z}\bigr)
are isomorphic and the latter is freely generated.

Finally, notice that the solutions to (
C.2
) for
c
form a torsor over
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})
. We can thus choose an arbitrary particular solution
c^{*}
such that
dc^{*}=-\beta(b^{\prime})
and set
c=c^{*}+c^{\prime}
with
c^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})
. In the following, with a slight abuse of notation, we will simply indicate a sum over
c
.

Now we provide a more detailed account of the manipulations presented in the main text. We start with the theories
\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}
describing vacua with partial breaking/confinement of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry. Their partition function is in (
3.20
). As mentioned in the main text, the SPT phase is evaluated in the preserved subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
. This stems from the fact that the
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}}
gauge theory has support on trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}}
backgrounds,
i.e.
, its partition function is a delta function imposing
B=k^{\prime}B^{\prime}
and in that case the partition function reduces to

Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}](k^{\prime}B^{\prime})=\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})\rvert}\;\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2k}\int\mathfrak{P}(B^{\prime})\biggr)\;,

(C.4)

which is a well defined
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
SPT phase. The extreme cases are a pure
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory with partition function
Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N}]=Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N|1}^{0}]
, and
\text{SPT}_{\ell}
with partition function
Z[\text{SPT}_{\ell}]=Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N|N}^{\ell}]
.

Whenever
{\rm gcd}(\ell,k)=1
, there is an alternative expression:

Z[\mathbb{Z}_{N|k}^{\ell}](B)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\rvert}}\sum_{b\,\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})}\exp\biggl(-\frac{2\pi i\ell^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}}{2k}\int\mathfrak{P}(b)+\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int b\cup B\biggr)\;,

(C.5)

where
\ell^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}
is the inverse of
\ell
mod
k
. In order to show that the above expression equals (
3.20
), one decomposes
b=kb^{\prime}+b^{\prime\prime}
with
b^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})
(as previously explained, we assume a simply connected spacetime). Summing over
b^{\prime}
yields a delta function
\delta_{H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})}(B)
, while completing the square and summing over
b^{\prime\prime}
gives the same SPT phase as in (
3.20
).

Let us now describe some of the computations from Section
3.2
. In particular, the first two entries in equation (
3.28
) follow almost by definition. In order to derive the third one, we need to make use of the following result:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})\rvert}}\;\sum_{c\,\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})}\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi ip}{2k}\int\mathfrak{P}(c)\biggr)=1

(C.6)

when
X
is a simply-connected spin manifold
[
71
]
. We therefore have, assuming
\gcd(\ell,N)=1
:

\displaystyle[\sigma\,\text{SPT}_{\ell}](B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\rvert}}\;\sum_{b\,\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})}e^{\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2N}\!\int\mathfrak{P}(b)\,+\,\frac{2\pi i}{N}\!\int\!b\,\cup B}

(C.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\rvert}}\;\sum_{b\,\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})}e^{\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2N}\!\int\mathfrak{P}\left(b+\ell^{\raisebox{0.43054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}B\right)\,-\,\frac{2\pi i\ell^{\raisebox{0.43054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}}{2N}\!\int\mathfrak{P}(B)}=\text{SPT}_{-\ell^{\raisebox{0.43054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}}(B)\;.

To establish the last equality one uses (
C.6
). Here
\ell^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}
is the inverse modulo
N
, which exists due to the assumption
\gcd(\ell,N)=1
. Let us introduce the generic notation
(\ell\,)^{-1}_{x}
to denote the multiplicative inverse mod
x
.
When this condition fails, we define
p=\gcd(\ell,N)
and split the dynamical field as
b=(N/p)\,c+b^{\prime}
. As explained at the beginning of this section, the following manipulations are perfectly justified as long as the spacetime is restricted to a simply-connected four-manifold.
Given that, the sum over connections reads:

\displaystyle[\sigma\,\text{SPT}_{\ell}](B)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lvert H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})\rvert}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\,\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{p})\\
b^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N/p})\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2N}\!\int\!\mathfrak{P}(b^{\prime})+\frac{2\pi i}{N}\!\int b^{\prime}\cup B+\frac{2\pi i}{p}\!\int\!c\cup B\biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{p})|}{\sqrt{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})|}}\sum_{b^{\prime}\in H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{N/p})}\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell/p}{2N/p}\!\int\!\mathfrak{P}(b^{\prime})+\frac{2\pi i}{N/p}\int b^{\prime}\cup B/p\biggr)\,\delta_{H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{p})}(B)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl(-\frac{2\pi i(\ell/p)^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}_{N/p}}{2Np}\int\!\mathfrak{P}(B)\biggr)\,Z[\mathbb{Z}_{p}](B)=Z\biggl[\mathbb{Z}_{N|N/p}^{(\ell/p)_{N/p}^{\raisebox{0.43054pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}}\biggr](B)\;,

(C.8)

thus leading to the last entry in (
3.28
). In the first line we used the fact that
\mathfrak{P}
is a quadratic form,
\mathfrak{P}(A+B)=\mathfrak{P}(A)+\mathfrak{P}(B)+2A\cup B
, to drop the
c
dependence. In the second line we performed the sum over
c
and dropped terms which are trivial on the support of the delta function. Finally setting
B=pB^{\prime}
to solve the constraint, we performed the final sum over
b^{\prime}
using (
C.6
) and reinstated the old variable
B
.

Let us now consider the general case and prove (
3.29
). Setting
N=kk^{\prime}
,
p={\rm gcd}(\ell,k)
, we want to compute

\displaystyle\sigma\,Z\Bigl[\mathbb{Z}^{(\ell)}_{N|k}\Bigr](B)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})|}{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})|}}\sum_{c\,\in\,H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})}\!\!\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{2Nk^{\prime}}\int\mathfrak{P}(c)+\frac{2\pi i}{N}\!\int\!c\cup B\biggr)\,\delta_{H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})}(c)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k^{\prime}})|}{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{N})|}}\sum_{\tilde{c}\,\in\,H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})}\!\!\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell}{k}\!\int\!\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{c})+\frac{2\pi i}{k}\!\int\!\tilde{c}\cup B\biggr)\;.

(C.9)

To go to the second line we solved the delta function constraint by
c=k^{\prime}\tilde{c}
. We are now in a familiar situation and we further decompose
\tilde{c}=(k/p)c^{\prime}+c^{\prime\prime}
so that
c^{\prime}
appears only linearly and gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
delta function enforcing
B=pB^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle(\ref{eqn C13})

\displaystyle=\frac{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{p})|}{\sqrt{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{k})|}}\sum_{c^{\prime\prime}\in H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{Z}_{k/p})}\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i\ell/p}{k/p}\int\mathfrak{P}(c^{\prime\prime})+\frac{2\pi i}{k/p}\int c^{\prime\prime}\cup B^{\prime}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{|H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{p})|}\exp\biggl(-\frac{2\pi i(\ell/p)^{-1}_{k/p}}{k/p}\int\mathfrak{P}(B^{\prime})\biggr)\;.

(C.10)

Here we used that
H^{2}(X)
is torsion-free and the sum (
C.6
) to rearrange the prefactors. We can now reinstate the variable
B=pB^{\prime}
and the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
gauge-theory factor explicitly to find:

\sigma\,Z\Bigl[\mathbb{Z}^{(\ell)}_{N|k}\Bigr](B)=\exp\biggl(-\frac{2\pi ik^{\prime}(\ell/p)_{k/p}^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}}{2Np}\int\mathfrak{P}(B)\biggr)\,Z[\mathbb{Z}_{p}](B)=Z\Bigl[\mathbb{Z}_{N|N/p}^{\tilde{\ell}}\Bigr](B)

(C.11)

with
\tilde{\ell}\equiv-k^{\prime}(\ell/p)^{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}_{k/p}
, thus proving equation (
3.29
).
